    "newString": "\\includegraphics[width=0.93\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{The shifting bottleneck in LLM judge panels. \\textbf{(a)}~At $K{=}3$, one compromised judge flips the majority---$\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates. \\textbf{(b)}~A predictor shift emerges at $K^* \\in (3,5)$. \\textbf{(c)}~At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise multiple independent judges---$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor.}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{Overview of our evaluation framework. Given $N$ candidate LLM judges, we construct $K$-judge panels (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}), subject them to six adversarial attack types (\\S3), and assess vulnerability through two complementary metrics: $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link individual susceptibility) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (effective independent channels via mutual information). \\textbf{(a)}~At small $K$, a single compromised judge flips the majority---$\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates. \\textbf{(b)}~A predictor shift emerges at $K^* \\in (3,5)$: $\\eta_{\\max}$'s influence declines while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s rises. \\textbf{(c)}~At large $K$, the adversary must compromise multiple independent judges---structural diversity ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) becomes the dominant predictor.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have become the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, an approach inspired by Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates (ASR) from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation, yet this framing conflates two distinct sources of robustness: the quality of individual judges and the independence among them.\n\nThe conflation matters because the BFT analogy breaks down in the LLM judge setting. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes from within; evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs from without, and shared pretraining corpora create correlated failure modes that violate the independence assumption underlying majority-vote guarantees \\citep{schneier2000secrets}. No existing work disentangles diversity from individual robustness across panel sizes, so the mechanism behind observed ASR reductions remains unclear.\n\nWe address this gap by decomposing panel vulnerability into two predictors: weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$, the maximum individual attack success rate in a panel) and effective independent channels ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, a spectral measure of judge independence). Across all 12 conditions (two benchmarks $\\times$ six attacks), $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s ordinary least-squares (OLS) coefficient is positive (12/12), directionally consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. However, the significance of this association is method-sensitive (0 to 12 of 12 across inference approaches), and a previously uncharacterized transition emerges: at $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute (bootstrap significance: $\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor (7/12 at $K{=}5$, 8/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\\eta_{\\max}$ sharply attenuates (2/12, then 1/12).\n\nControlled experiments across 15 models (9 API, 6 local quantized), $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, six attack types, and two benchmarks, Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) and the AI2 Reasoning Challenge (ARC), confirm this predictor shift. Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); adaptive attack reduces predictability to $R^2 = 0.275$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}).\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Diversity--vulnerability association.} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions (12/12 under OLS and generalized additive model (GAM) bootstrap), but the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) limits causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\\% of conditions (25/36), though fractional logit confirms the positive association in most conditions ($K{=}3$: 9/12; $K{=}5$: 11/12; $K{=}7$: 12/12) (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Panel-size-dependent predictor shift.} At $K{=}3$, both $\\eta_{\\max}$ (3/12) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (4/12) contribute under bootstrap (co-determination); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor (7/12 at $K{=}5$, 8/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\\eta_{\\max}$ sharply attenuates (2/12, then 1/12) (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).\n\\item \\textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), so the defense is partial rather than complete (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nOur findings are descriptive; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) limits causal claims.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

          "+\\includegraphics[width=0.93\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01WNsQds66sauQTmRi6T9gXp`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation.}
4	LLM judges gained traction after \citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy), motivating multi-judge panels; \citet{raina2024judgerobust} quantified robustness gaps under universal adversarial attacks, \citet{maloyan2025investigating} demonstrated prompt injection vulnerabilities in LLM-as-a-judge systems, and \citet{stureborg2024inconsistent} and \citet{ye2024justice} further catalogued inconsistency and bias in LLM evaluators. Aggregating noisy annotators traces back to \citet{dawid1979maximum}; \citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias driving within-family correlations. These frameworks assume benign conditions; we test whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation. \citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\% to 10--19\%, but did not disentangle diversity from individual robustness. \citet{tamas2025benchmarking} benchmark adversarial risks in multi-agent LLM systems but focus on agent coordination rather than judge panel composition. \citet{qian2026collabeval} propose CollabEval, a three-phase collaborative evaluation framework (independent assessment, multi-round discussion, consensus judgment) that outperforms single-judge baselines; their work assumes benign conditions and does not examine adversarial manipulation or the diversity--vulnerability tradeoff we characterize.
5	
6	\paragraph{Condorcet Jury Theorem.}
7	\citet{condorcet1785essai} proved that majority voting accuracy converges to 1 when per-voter accuracy exceeds 0.5; \citet{ladha1992condorcet} extended the theorem to correlated voters, showing that positive correlation weakens the convergence guarantee. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) explicitly assumes independent, non-adversarial errors; its failure under adaptive adversaries is trivially expected \citep{tramer2018ensemble,kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}. We focus on the \emph{panel-size-dependent structure} of this vulnerability: the predictor shift from co-determination at $K{=}3$ to $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance at $K{\geq}5$.
8	
9	\paragraph{Ensemble Diversity Theory.}
10	\citet{krogh1995neural} proved that ensemble error equals average member error minus a diversity term; \citet{brown2005managing} showed diversity reduces error only when members maintain sufficiently low bias. In our setting, weaker judges introduce bias large enough to offset diversity benefits. \citet{kuncheva2003measures} found that no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicted ensemble improvement; our $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a $K$-dependent pattern: at $K{=}3$, OLS significance (12/12) attenuates under bootstrap (4/12); at $K{\geq}5$, it survives bootstrap inference (7/12). Recent information-theoretic frameworks \citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.
11	
12	\paragraph{Adversarial Robustness and Transferability.}
13	Adversarial vulnerability was formalized by \citet{goodfellow2015adversarial}, with transferability demonstrated by \citet{papernot2016transferability,papernot2017practical}. \citet{tramer2018ensemble} introduced ensemble adversarial training but did not eliminate cross-model transfer. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision, but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection. Three differences motivate our empirical approach: adversaries use natural-language manipulation rather than gradient perturbations, panel decisions are discrete votes, and aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter.
14	
15	\paragraph{BFT and Security Games.}
16	Classical BFT \citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical} tolerates up to $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but assumes non-strategic faults; \citet{schneier2000secrets} articulated the weakest-link principle that an attacker exploits the single most vulnerable component. Our framework adopts the Stackelberg structure of \citet{tambe2011security}. Correlated LLM judge errors \citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate BFT's independence assumption, motivating our empirical decomposition. \citet{kuai2026independent} propose the Difficulty-Weighted Behavioral Entanglement Index (DBEI), weighting agreement by item difficulty; $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ captures a complementary dimension via pairwise MI, providing an information-theoretic measure of panel redundancy. Mitigation strategies include DBEI-based reweighting, Bayesian calibration, and credibility scoring \citep{ebrahimi2025adversary}.
17	
18	\paragraph{Information-Theoretic Evaluation.}
19	Information-theoretic approaches address statistical validity \citep{robertson2025stepwise,feng2026noisy}, attack costs \citep{kaneko2025bits}, sequential selection \citep{jung2026logarithmic}, and truthfulness \citep{chen2025incentivizing,qiu2026truthfulness}, but assume honest judges. We address adversarial manipulation interacting with multi-judge composition.
20	
```

In our setting, weaker judges introduce bias large enough to offset diversity benefits. \\citet{kuncheva2003measures} found that no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicted ensemble improvement; our $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a $K$-dependent pattern: at $K{=}3$, OLS significance (12/12) attenuates under bootstrap (4/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, it survives bootstrap inference (7/12). Recent information-theoretic frameworks \\citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial Robustness and Transferability.}\nAdversarial vulnerability was formalized by \\citet{goodfellow2015adversarial}, with transferability demonstrated by \\citet{papernot2016transferability,papernot2017practical}. \\citet{tramer2018ensemble} introduced ensemble adversarial training but did not eliminate cross-model transfer. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \\citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \\citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision, but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection. Three differences motivate our empirical approach: adversaries use natural-language manipulation rather than gradient perturbations, panel decisions are discrete votes, and aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter.\n\n\\paragraph{BFT and Security Games.}\nClassical BFT \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical} tolerates up to $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but assumes non-strategic faults; \\citet{schneier2000secrets} articulated the weakest-link principle that an attacker exploits the single most vulnerable component. Our framework adopts the Stackelberg structure of \\citet{tambe2011security}. Correlated LLM judge errors \\citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate BFT's independence assumption, motivating our empirical decomposition. \\citet{kuai2026independent} propose the Difficulty-Weighted Behavioral Entanglement Index (DBEI), weighting agreement by item difficulty; $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ captures a complementary dimension via pairwise MI, providing an information-theoretic measure of panel redundancy. Mitigation strategies include DBEI-based reweighting, Bayesian calibration, and credibility scoring \\citep{ebrahimi2025adversary}.\n\n\\paragraph{Information-Theoretic Evaluation.}\nInformation-theoretic approaches address statistical validity \\citep{robertson2025stepwise,feng2026noisy}, attack costs \\citep{kaneko2025bits}, sequential selection \\citep{jung2026logarithmic}, and truthfulness \\citep{chen2025incentivizing,qiu2026truthfulness}, but assume honest judges. We address adversarial manipulation interacting with multi-judge composition.\n",

      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 20
    }

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation.}\nLLM judges gained traction after \\citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \\citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy), motivating multi-judge panels; \\citet{raina2024judgerobust} quantified robustness gaps under universal adversarial attacks, \\citet{maloyan2025investigating} demonstrated prompt injection vulnerabilities in LLM-as-a-judge systems, and \\citet{stureborg2024inconsistent} and \\citet{ye2024justice} further catalogued inconsistency and bias in LLM evaluators. Aggregating noisy annotators traces back to \\citet{dawid1979maximum}; \\citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \\citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias driving within-family correlations. These frameworks assume benign conditions; we test whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation. \\citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\%, but did not disentangle diversity from individual robustness. \\citet{tamas2025benchmarking} benchmark adversarial risks in multi-agent LLM systems but focus on agent coordination rather than judge panel composition. \\citet{qian2026collabeval} propose CollabEval, a three-phase collaborative evaluation framework (independent assessment, multi-round discussion, consensus judgment) that outperforms single-judge baselines; their work assumes benign conditions and does not examine adversarial manipulation or the diversity--vulnerability tradeoff we characterize.\n\n\\paragraph{Condorcet Jury Theorem.}\n\\citet{condorcet1785essai} proved that majority voting accuracy converges to 1 when per-voter accuracy exceeds 0.5; \\citet{ladha1992condorcet} extended the theorem to correlated voters, showing that positive correlation weakens the convergence guarantee. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) explicitly assumes independent, non-adversarial errors; its failure under adaptive adversaries is trivially expected \\citep{tramer2018ensemble,kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}. We focus on the \\emph{panel-size-dependent structure} of this vulnerability: the predictor shift from co-determination at $K{=}3$ to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance at $K{\\geq}5$.\n\n\\paragraph{Ensemble Diversity Theory.}\n\\citet{krogh1995neural} proved that ensemble error equals average member error minus a diversity term; \\citet{brown2005managing} showed diversity reduces error only when members maintain sufficiently low bias. In our setting, weaker judges introduce bias large enough to offset diversity benefits. \\citet{kuncheva2003measures} found that no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicted ensemble improvement; our $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a $K$-dependent pattern: at $K{=}3$, OLS significance (12/12) attenuates under bootstrap (4/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, it survives bootstrap inference (7/12). Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \\citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \\citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision, but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection. Three differences motivate our empirical approach: adversaries use natural-language manipulation rather than gradient perturbations, panel decisions are discrete votes, and aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter.\n\n\\paragraph{BFT and Security Games.}\nClassical BFT \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical} tolerates up to $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but assumes non-strategic faults; \\citet{schneier2000secrets} articulated the weakest-link principle that an attacker exploits the single most vulnerable component. Our framework adopts the Stackelberg structure of \\citet{tambe2011security}. Correlated LLM judge errors \\citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate BFT's independence assumption, motivating our empirical decomposition. \\citet{kuai2026independent} propose the Difficulty-Weighted Behavioral Entanglement Index (DBEI), weighting agreement by item difficulty; $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ captures a complementary dimension via pairwise MI, providing an information-theoretic measure of panel redundancy. Mitigation strategies include DBEI-based reweighting, Bayesian calibration, and credibility scoring \\citep{ebrahimi2025adversary}.\n\n\\paragraph{Information-Theoretic Evaluation.}\nInformation-theoretic approaches address statistical validity \\citep{robertson2025stepwise,feng2026noisy}, attack costs \\citep{kaneko2025bits}, sequential selection \\citep{jung2026logarithmic}, and truthfulness \\citep{chen2025incentivizing,qiu2026truthfulness}, but assume honest judges. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \\citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \\citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision, but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection. Three differences motivate our empirical approach: adversaries use natural-language manipulation rather than gradient perturbations, panel decisions are discrete votes, and aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter.",
    "newString": "Adversarial vulnerability was formalized by \\citet{goodfellow2015adversarial}, with transferability demonstrated by \\citet{papernot2016transferability,papernot2017practical}. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \\citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \\citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection. Our setting differs: adversaries use natural-language manipulation rather than gradient perturbations, and aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation.}\nLLM judges gained traction after \\citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \\citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy), motivating multi-judge panels; \\citet{raina2024judgerobust} quantified robustness gaps under adversarial attacks, and \\citet{maloyan2025investigating} demonstrated prompt injection vulnerabilities. Aggregating noisy annotators traces back to \\citet{dawid1979maximum}; \\citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \\citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias driving within-family correlations. \\citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\%, but did not disentangle diversity from individual robustness. \\citet{tamas2025benchmarking} benchmark adversarial risks in multi-agent systems but focus on agent coordination; \\citet{qian2026collabeval} propose collaborative multi-phase evaluation but assume benign conditions. We test whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation.\n\n\\paragraph{Condorcet Jury Theorem.}\n\\citet{condorcet1785essai} proved that majority voting accuracy converges to 1 when per-voter accuracy exceeds 0.5; \\citet{ladha1992condorcet} extended the theorem to correlated voters, showing that positive correlation weakens the convergence guarantee. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) explicitly assumes independent, non-adversarial errors; its failure under adaptive adversaries is trivially expected \\citep{tramer2018ensemble,kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}. We focus on the \\emph{panel-size-dependent structure} of this vulnerability: the predictor shift from co-determination at $K{=}3$ to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance at $K{\\geq}5$.\n\n\\paragraph{Ensemble Diversity Theory.}\n\\citet{krogh1995neural} proved that ensemble error equals average member error minus a diversity term; \\citet{brown2005managing} showed diversity reduces error only when members maintain sufficiently low bias. In our setting, weaker judges introduce bias large enough to offset diversity benefits. \\citet{kuncheva2003measures} found that no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicted ensemble improvement; our $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a $K$-dependent pattern: at $K{=}3$, OLS significance (12/12) attenuates under bootstrap (4/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, it survives bootstrap inference (7/12). Recent information-theoretic frameworks \\citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial Robustness and Transferability.}\nAdversarial vulnerability was formalized by \\citet{goodfellow2015adversarial}, with transferability demonstrated by \\citet{papernot2016transferability,papernot2017practical}. \\citet{tramer2018ensemble} introduced ensemble adversarial training but did not eliminate cross-model transfer. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \\citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \\citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision, but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection. Three differences motivate our empirical approach: adversaries use natural-language manipulation rather than gradient perturbations, panel decisions are discrete votes, and aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter.\n\n\\paragraph{BFT and Security Games.}\nClassical BFT \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical} tolerates up to $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but assumes non-strategic faults; \\citet{schneier2000secrets} articulated the weakest-link principle that an attacker exploits the single most vulnerable component. Our framework adopts the Stackelberg structure of \\citet{tambe2011security}. Correlated LLM judge errors \\citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate BFT's independence assumption, motivating our empirical decomposition. \\citet{kuai2026independent} propose the Difficulty-Weighted Behavioral Entanglement Index (DBEI), weighting agreement by item difficulty; $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ captures a complementary dimension via pairwise MI, providing an information-theoretic measure of panel redundancy. Mitigation strategies include DBEI-based reweighting, Bayesian calibration, and credibility scoring \\citep{ebrahimi2025adversary}.\n\n\\paragraph{Information-Theoretic Evaluation.}\nInformation-theoretic approaches address statistical validity \\citep{robertson2025stepwise,feng2026noisy}, attack costs \\citep{kaneko2025bits}, sequential selection \\citep{jung2026logarithmic}, and truthfulness \\citep{chen2025incentivizing,qiu2026truthfulness}, but assume honest judges. We address adversarial manipulation interacting with multi-judge composition.\n",

```text
1	\section{Analysis}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	\paragraph{Predictor Shift and Direction.}
5	At $K{=}3$, a single compromised judge flips the majority ($t{=}2$), making both predictors relevant. At $K{\geq}5$, no single judge is pivotal; $\eta_{\max}$'s contribution collapses while high-$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ panels present more distinct attack surfaces ($K^* \in (3,5)$). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction is positive in all 12 conditions (OLS and GAM bootstrap 12/12), but significance is method-sensitive (0--12/12; bootstrap 4/12 at $K{=}3$, 7/12 at $K{=}5$). No magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$; Appendix~\ref{app:bft_reversal}).
6	
7	\begin{figure}[t]
8	\centering
9	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_keff_eta_scatter.pdf}
10	\caption{Panel-level correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ at $K{=}5$ ($n{=}3{,}003$ panels). Full model pool (gray, $r{=}0.81$) exhibits strong collinearity; capability-matched Pool~A (orange, $r{=}0.50$, 126 panels) shows reduced confounding.}
11	\label{fig:keff_eta_scatter}
12	\end{figure}
13	
14	\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound: A Fundamental Limitation.}
15	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ correlate at $r{=}0.70$--$0.81$ (variance inflation factor, VIF${\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; Figure~\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \citep{cinelli2020making} shows the confound explains the association in 69\% of conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}), though fractional logit confirms the positive association (9/12, 11/12, 12/12 at $K{=}3,5,7$). Pool~A ($r{=}0.50$) raises the robustness value (RV) in 10/12 at $K{=}5$ despite max 30.1\% power (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}); cross-benchmark instrumental variable (IV) analysis provides additional identification (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_benchmark}), though causal attribution requires capability-controlled designs.
16	
17	\paragraph{API-Only vs.\ Full Pool.}
18	Restricting to 9 API models removes the AWQ confound but increases collinearity ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$), reversing the pattern: $\eta_{\max}$ retains significance (8/12) while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ drops (2--3/12; Table~\ref{tab:robustness_checks}). The six local AWQ models (25--54\% accuracy) drive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ variation but also introduce the confound (Appendix~\ref{app:random_subsample}).
19	
20	\begin{figure}[t]
21	\centering
22	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_panel_design.pdf}
23	\caption{Panel ASR under three selection strategies. $\min(\eta_{\max})$ achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions, confirming robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.}
24	\label{fig:panel_design}
25	\end{figure}
26	
27	\begin{figure}[t]
28	\centering
29	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_aggregation_gradient.pdf}
30	\caption{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient distribution across aggregation methods ($K{=}3$). Majority and weighted voting: uniformly positive (12+/0$-$); Bayesian calibration: reduced predictability ($R^2{=}0.087$, 5+/1$-$).}
31	\label{fig:aggregation}
32	\end{figure}
33	
34	\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}
35	Aggregation rules influence predictability but not $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (Figure~\ref{fig:aggregation}); at $K{=}5$ all four methods converge to 7/12, while at $K{=}7$ Bayesian calibration neutralizes it (0/12; Appendices~\ref{app:aggregation_k5},~\ref{app:aggregation_k7}). Robustness-first selection ($\min \eta_{\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in all conditions (Figure~\ref{fig:panel_design}); clean $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is stable under 4/6 attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}).
36	
37	\paragraph{Bayesian Non-Monotonic Behavior.}
38	Bayesian calibration's $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance is non-monotonic (6/12, 7/12, 0/12 at $K{=}3,5,7$): at large $K$, the posterior concentrates on 2--3 accurate judges, compressing $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ variation (Appendix~\ref{app:aggregation_k7}).
39	
40	\paragraph{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\ Alternative Diversity Measures.}
41	$\kappa$-diversity \citep{kuncheva2003measures} outperforms $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (8/12 vs.\ 4/12) but falls behind at $K{\geq}5$ (6--7/12 vs.\ 7--8/12) without the predictor shift ($8 \to 7 \to 6$; Appendix~\ref{app:alt_diversity}). Shannon MI entropy shows no shift either ($4 \to 4 \to 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:shannon_entropy}).
42	
```

```text
39	\subsection{Benchmarks and Attack Types}
40	
41	Two benchmarks test distinct evaluation capabilities: MMLU \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}, a knowledge-intensive multiple-choice benchmark spanning 57 subjects, and ARC \citep{clark2018arc}, a reasoning-intensive science question benchmark. We sample 300 items from each benchmark. MMLU probes factual recall and broad domain knowledge; ARC requires multi-step scientific reasoning. All API calls use temperature~0 for deterministic outputs; local models are served with the same temperature setting (a pilot study confirms temperature has negligible effect on $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$; Appendix~\ref{app:temperature}). Attacked variants are generated by programmatically applying each manipulation to the candidate text, with a fixed random seed for reproducibility.
42	
43	Each benchmark is paired with six attack types that target different judge vulnerabilities: (1)~\textbf{prompt injection}, adversarial instructions embedded in candidate text that override the evaluation prompt \citep{perez2022ignore,greshake2023not}; (2)~\textbf{sycophancy}, responses engineered to agree with or flatter the judge \citep{wang2023fair}; (3)~\textbf{score manipulation}, explicit numerical cues (e.g., ``this answer deserves a 10/10'') injected into the candidate to anchor scoring; (4)~\textbf{verbosity bias}, padding correct answers with excessive irrelevant detail to exploit length preferences \citep{zheng2023judging}; (5)~\textbf{position bias}, reordering candidate answers to place the target in the position judges systematically favor \citep{wang2023fair}; and (6)~\textbf{authority bias}, prefacing candidate text with fabricated credentials or citations to inflate perceived credibility. The combination of 2 benchmarks and 6 attacks produces 12 experimental conditions, each evaluated across all 455 panels (implementation details and per-attack parameters in Appendix~\ref{app:attack_details}, Table~\ref{tab:attack-params}).
44	
45	\subsection{Vulnerability Metrics}
46	
47	Per-judge vulnerability under attack $a$ is the fraction of items where judge $j$ is fooled: $\eta_j(a) = \Pr(X_j^a {=} 1)$, estimated empirically over the item set. The weakest-link vulnerability $\eta_{\max}(a) = \max_{j \in \mathcal{P}} \eta_j(a)$ captures the most susceptible member of each panel. The panel attack success rate $\mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a)$ is the fraction of items where at least $t$ judges are simultaneously fooled. Panel diversity is quantified by $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\ref{eq:keff}), computed from pairwise mutual information between judges' three-category winner labels (model~A wins, model~B wins, tie) on clean (unattacked) data. MI is estimated via the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow bias correction. The term $\exp(-2\,\mathrm{MI}(i,j))$ provides an information-theoretic measure of pairwise alienation: it equals 1 when judges are independent ($\mathrm{MI}{=}0$) and approaches 0 as dependence grows. Under a Gaussian copula, this reduces to $1 - \rho_{ij}^2$, yielding the closed form $K_{\mathrm{eff}} = K - \frac{1}{K}\sum_{i}\sum_{j \neq i} \rho_{ij}^2$ \citep{nelsen2006copulas}; however, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s validity does not depend on this parametric equivalence. Goodness-of-fit testing rejects the Gaussian copula for 97--100\% of judge pairs (Appendix~\ref{app:copula_gof}), yet the plug-in MI and closed-form rankings agree closely (within-$K$ Spearman $\rho{\approx}0.86$), confirming that $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is robust to copula misspecification as a ranking metric. Together, $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ serve as the two predictors in the regression framework below: $\eta_{\max}$ indexes the weakest-link channel and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ indexes the diversity channel. Robustness of $\eta_{\max}$ under partial panel information is analyzed in Appendix~\ref{app:oracle}.
48	
```

Panel diversity is quantified by $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\\ref{eq:keff}), computed from pairwise mutual information between judges' three-category winner labels (model~A wins, model~B wins, tie) on clean (unattacked) data. MI is estimated via the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow bias correction. The term $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI}(i,j))$ provides an information-theoretic measure of pairwise alienation: it equals 1 when judges are independent ($\\mathrm{MI}{=}0$) and approaches 0 as dependence grows. Under a Gaussian copula, this reduces to $1 - \\rho_{ij}^2$, yielding the closed form $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} = K - \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i}\\sum_{j \\neq i} \\rho_{ij}^2$ \\citep{nelsen2006copulas}; however, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s validity does not depend on this parametric equivalence. Goodness-of-fit testing rejects the Gaussian copula for 97--100\\% of judge pairs (Appendix~\\ref{app:copula_gof}), yet the plug-in MI and closed-form rankings agree closely (within-$K$ Spearman $\\rho{\\approx}0.86$), confirming that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is robust to copula misspecification as a ranking metric. Together, $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ serve as the two predictors in the regression framework below: $\\eta_{\\max}$ indexes the weakest-link channel and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ indexes the diversity channel. Robustness of $\\eta_{\\max}$ under partial panel information is analyzed in Appendix~\\ref{app:oracle}.\n",
      "numLines": 10,
      "startLine": 39,
      "totalLines": 79
    }
  },

    "oldString": "Panel diversity is quantified by $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\\ref{eq:keff}), computed from pairwise mutual information between judges' three-category winner labels (model~A wins, model~B wins, tie) on clean (unattacked) data. MI is estimated via the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow bias correction. The term $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI}(i,j))$ provides an information-theoretic measure of pairwise alienation: it equals 1 when judges are independent ($\\mathrm{MI}{=}0$) and approaches 0 as dependence grows. Under a Gaussian copula, this reduces to $1 - \\rho_{ij}^2$, yielding the closed form $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} = K - \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i}\\sum_{j \\neq i} \\rho_{ij}^2$ \\citep{nelsen2006copulas}; however, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s validity does not depend on this parametric equivalence. Goodness-of-fit testing rejects the Gaussian copula for 97--100\\% of judge pairs (Appendix~\\ref{app:copula_gof}), yet the plug-in MI and closed-form rankings agree closely (within-$K$ Spearman $\\rho{\\approx}0.86$), confirming that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is robust to copula misspecification as a ranking metric. Together, $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ serve as the two predictors in the regression framework below: $\\eta_{\\max}$ indexes the weakest-link channel and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ indexes the diversity channel. Robustness of $\\eta_{\\max}$ under partial panel information is analyzed in Appendix~\\ref{app:oracle}.",

MI is estimated via the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow bias correction. Goodness-of-fit testing rejects the Gaussian copula for 97--100\\% of judge pairs (Appendix~\\ref{app:copula_gof}), yet the plug-in MI and closed-form rankings agree closely (within-$K$ Spearman $\\rho{\\approx}0.86$), confirming that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is robust to copula misspecification as a ranking metric. ```text
48	
49	\subsection{Aggregation Methods}
50	
51	Beyond the default majority vote, we evaluate three alternative aggregation rules to test whether the diversity-vulnerability association persists under different decision mechanisms. The aggregation comparison uses the same log-log regression specification as the primary analysis (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}).
52	
53	\paragraph{Majority Vote.} Accept if $\sum_{j=1}^K \mathbf{1}[J_j \text{ approves}] \geq \lceil K/2 \rceil$ (baseline).
54	
55	\paragraph{Accuracy-Weighted Vote.} Weight judges by clean-data accuracy: $w_j \propto \mathrm{acc}_j$, $\sum_j w_j = 1$; accept if $\sum_j w_j \cdot \mathbf{1}[J_j \text{ approves}] > 0.5$.
56	
57	\paragraph{Threshold Vote (Unanimity).} Accept only if all $K$ judges approve ($t = K$).
58	
59	\paragraph{Bayesian Calibration.} Compute $P(\text{correct} \mid \mathbf{v}) \propto P(\text{correct}) \prod_j P(v_j \mid \text{correct})$ using each judge's confusion matrix on clean data; accept if the posterior exceeds 0.5.
60	
61	\subsection{Statistical Framework}
62	\label{sec:stat_framework}
63	
64	To disentangle the contributions of panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:
65	\begin{equation}
66	\label{eq:regression}
67	\log(\mathrm{ASR} + \epsilon) = \alpha + \beta_1 \log(K_{\mathrm{eff}}) + \beta_2 \log(\eta_{\max} + \epsilon),
68	\end{equation}
69	with $\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values (sensitivity to $\epsilon$ is analyzed in Appendix~\ref{app:epsilon}). Standardized coefficients $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = \hat{\beta} \cdot \mathrm{SD}(\log x) / \mathrm{SD}(\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) correction for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate (FDR) per predictor across the 12 conditions.
70	
71	To assess relative predictor importance beyond regression coefficients, we perform dominance analysis \citep{budescu1993dominance}. This method decomposes $R^2$ by averaging each predictor's incremental $R^2$ contribution across all possible subsets of predictors. A predictor is \emph{dominant} if its average contribution exceeds that of the competing predictor in every subset ordering. With two predictors, dominance reduces to comparing each predictor's solo $R^2$ with its incremental $R^2$ conditional on the other.
72	
73	To test whether $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect direction varies by attack type, we fit generalized additive models (GAMs) with smooth terms for both predictors, allowing nonlinear partial effects. The GAM partial effect of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is then examined separately for targeted attacks (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) and shared-bias attacks (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias).
74	
75	\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}
76	\label{sec:robustness_method}
77	
```

75	\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}
76	\label{sec:robustness_method}
77	
78	The 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We correct for this using clustered standard errors (SE; CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts, 3-position max-$p$), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. Cluster counts (13, ${\leq}11$, ${\leq}9$ at $K{=}3,5,7$) fall below the ${\geq}20$ threshold of \citet{cameron2008bootstrap}; we use Rademacher weights following \citet{mackinnon2018wild}. The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (4/12 significant) may partly reflect insufficient cluster count; under crossed random effects correction (multi-membership CRVE, $t(14)$; Appendix~\ref{app:crossed_re}), $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains 8/12 at $K{\geq}5$ while $K{=}3$ attenuates further (0/12). We use the log-log specification rather than fractional response models \citep{papke1996econometric} or beta regression \citep{ferrari2004beta} because the log transform linearizes the multiplicative $K_{\mathrm{eff}} \cdot \eta_{\max}$ structure; fractional logit with clustered SE serves as an $\epsilon$-free robustness check (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}). Details are in Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_methods}.

    "newString": "Beyond majority vote ($\\geq \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ approvals), we evaluate three alternatives: \\emph{accuracy-weighted vote} ($w_j \\propto \\mathrm{acc}_j$; accept if $\\sum_j w_j \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] > 0.5$), \\emph{threshold vote} (unanimity, $t{=}K$), and \\emph{Bayesian calibration} ($P(\\text{correct} \\mid \\mathbf{v}) \\propto P(\\text{correct}) \\prod_j P(v_j \\mid \\text{correct})$; accept if posterior $> 0.5$).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Methodology}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Panel Construction}\n\nWe select 15 LLM judges spanning 6 provider families and 3 capability tiers, listed in Table~\\ref{tab:models}. Nine models are accessed via the OpenRouter API; six open-weight models are served locally using vLLM~0.21.0 with Activation-aware Weight Quantization (AWQ) INT4 on an NVIDIA RTX~6000 Ada (48\\,GB). API models are assigned to \\emph{frontier}, \\emph{mid-tier}, or \\emph{lightweight} tiers following the provider capability classification; all six local models fall into the lightweight tier, as INT4 quantization substantially reduces judge accuracy on the pairwise judgment task (Table~\\ref{tab:models}). We study three panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (all odd, majority-vote threshold $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$). Exhaustive enumeration yields $\\binom{15}{3} = 455$ panels at $K{=}3$, $\\binom{15}{5} = 3{,}003$ at $K{=}5$, and $\\binom{15}{7} = 6{,}435$ at $K{=}7$, covering every combination of families and tiers. Each panel evaluates all benchmark items under all attack types, producing a complete panel $\\times$ item $\\times$ attack tensor. The primary analysis uses $K{=}3$; \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k} extends to larger panels.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lllccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{Tier} & \\textbf{Deploy} & \\textbf{MMLU} & \\textbf{ARC} \\\\\n\\midrule\ngpt-5.5              & OpenAI    & frontier    & API   & .957 & .983 \\\\\ngemini-3.1-pro-preview & Google  & frontier    & API   & .955 & .987 \\\\\nclaude-opus-4-6       & Anthropic & frontier   & API   & .890 & .987 \\\\\ngemini-3.5-flash      & Google    & frontier   & API   & .968 & 1.00$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1               & OpenAI    & mid-tier   & API   & .837 & .967 \\\\\nclaude-sonnet-4-6     & Anthropic & mid-tier   & API   & .810 & .953 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1-mini          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .800 & .963 \\\\\ngpt-4.1-nano          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .633 & .853 \\\\\nclaude-3-haiku        & Anthropic & lightweight & API  & .767 & .450 \\\\\nmistral-large (123B)  & Mistral   & lightweight & local & .537 & .533 \\\\\nllama3.1-70b          & Llama     & lightweight & local & .523 & .520 \\\\\nllama3.1-8b           & Llama     & lightweight & local & .543 & .320 \\\\\nqwen2.5-72b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .333 & .410 \\\\\nqwen2.5-32b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .373 & .450 \\\\\nqwen2.5-14b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .253 & .240 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Judge models used in the study (clean accuracy on 300-item samples). API models accessed via OpenRouter (May 2026 snapshots); local models served with vLLM + AWQ INT4 quantization. $^\\dagger$\\,gemini-3.5-flash achieves perfect ARC accuracy but with 26.3\\% format error rate. Tier assignment follows provider capability classification (frontier $>$85\\% clean accuracy on both benchmarks, mid-tier 80--85\\%, lightweight ${\\leq}$80\\% or quantized); all six local AWQ INT4 models fall into lightweight, where quantization-induced accuracy reduction conflates genuine capability limitations with quantization damage (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).}\n\\label{tab:models}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Benchmarks and Attack Types}\n\nTwo benchmarks test distinct evaluation capabilities: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}, a knowledge-intensive multiple-choice benchmark spanning 57 subjects, and ARC \\citep{clark2018arc}, a reasoning-intensive science question benchmark. We sample 300 items from each. All models use temperature~0 for deterministic outputs (temperature has negligible effect on $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:temperature}).\n\nEach benchmark is paired with six attack types: (1)~\\textbf{prompt injection}, adversarial instructions overriding the evaluation prompt \\citep{perez2022ignore,greshake2023not}; (2)~\\textbf{sycophancy}, flattering language exploiting judge preferences \\citep{wang2023fair}; (3)~\\textbf{score manipulation}, explicit numerical cues anchoring scoring; (4)~\\textbf{verbosity bias}, padding with irrelevant detail \\citep{zheng2023judging}; (5)~\\textbf{position bias}, reordering to exploit positional preferences \\citep{wang2023fair}; and (6)~\\textbf{authority bias}, fabricated credentials inflating credibility. The 2 benchmarks $\\times$ 6 attacks produce 12 conditions, each evaluated across all 455 panels (details in Appendix~\\ref{app:attack_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Vulnerability Metrics}\n\nPer-judge vulnerability under attack $a$ is the fraction of items where judge $j$ is fooled: $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a {=} 1)$, estimated empirically over the item set. The weakest-link vulnerability $\\eta_{\\max}(a) = \\max_{j \\in \\mathcal{P}} \\eta_j(a)$ captures the most susceptible member of each panel. The panel attack success rate $\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$ is the fraction of items where at least $t$ judges are simultaneously fooled. Panel diversity is quantified by $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\\ref{eq:keff}), computed from pairwise MI between judges' three-category winner labels (model~A wins, model~B wins, tie) on clean data, using the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow bias correction. The $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI}(i,j))$ term measures pairwise alienation: 1 when independent, 0 under full dependence. Under a Gaussian copula this yields $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} = K - \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i}\\sum_{j \\neq i} \\rho_{ij}^2$ \\citep{nelsen2006copulas}; goodness-of-fit rejects the Gaussian copula for 97--100\\% of pairs, yet plug-in MI and closed-form rankings agree closely ($\\rho{\\approx}0.86$; Appendix~\\ref{app:copula_gof}). Together, $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link channel) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (diversity channel) serve as the two predictors in the regression framework below.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Methods}\n\nBeyond the default majority vote, we evaluate three alternative aggregation rules to test whether the diversity-vulnerability association persists under different decision mechanisms. The aggregation comparison uses the same log-log regression specification as the primary analysis (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}).\n\n\\paragraph{Majority Vote.} Accept if $\\sum_{j=1}^K \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] \\geq \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ (baseline).\n\n\\paragraph{Accuracy-Weighted Vote.} Weight judges by clean-data accuracy: $w_j \\propto \\mathrm{acc}_j$, $\\sum_j w_j = 1$; accept if $\\sum_j w_j \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] > 0.5$.\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold Vote (Unanimity).} Accept only if all $K$ judges approve ($t = K$).\n\n\\paragraph{Bayesian Calibration.} Compute $P(\\text{correct} \\mid \\mathbf{v}) \\propto P(\\text{correct}) \\prod_j P(v_j \\mid \\text{correct})$ using each judge's confusion matrix on clean data; accept if the posterior exceeds 0.5.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Framework}\n\\label{sec:stat_framework}\n\nTo disentangle the contributions of panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:regression}\n\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon) = \\alpha + \\beta_1 \\log(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}) + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max} + \\epsilon),\n\\end{equation}\nwith $\\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values (sensitivity to $\\epsilon$ is analyzed in Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon}). Standardized coefficients $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = \\hat{\\beta} \\cdot \\mathrm{SD}(\\log x) / \\mathrm{SD}(\\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) correction for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate (FDR) per predictor across the 12 conditions.\n\nTo assess relative predictor importance beyond regression coefficients, we perform dominance analysis \\citep{budescu1993dominance}. This method decomposes $R^2$ by averaging each predictor's incremental $R^2$ contribution across all possible subsets of predictors. A predictor is \\emph{dominant} if its average contribution exceeds that of the competing predictor in every subset ordering. With two predictors, dominance reduces to comparing each predictor's solo $R^2$ with its incremental $R^2$ conditional on the other.\n\nTo test whether $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect direction varies by attack type, we fit generalized additive models (GAMs) with smooth terms for both predictors, allowing nonlinear partial effects. The GAM partial effect of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is then examined separately for targeted attacks (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) and shared-bias attacks (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias).\n\n\\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}\n\\label{sec:robustness_method}\n\nThe 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We correct for this using clustered standard errors (SE; CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts, 3-position max-$p$), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. Cluster counts (13, ${\\leq}11$, ${\\leq}9$ at $K{=}3,5,7$) fall below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold of \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}; we use Rademacher weights following \\citet{mackinnon2018wild}. The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (4/12 significant) may partly reflect insufficient cluster count; under crossed random effects correction (multi-membership CRVE, $t(14)$; Appendix~\\ref{app:crossed_re}), $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains 8/12 at $K{\\geq}5$ while $K{=}3$ attenuates further (0/12). We use the log-log specification rather than fractional response models \\citep{papke1996econometric} or beta regression \\citep{ferrari2004beta} because the log transform linearizes the multiplicative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$ structure; fractional logit with clustered SE serves as an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). Details are in Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_methods}.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Methodology}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Panel Construction}\n\nWe select 15 LLM judges spanning 6 provider families and 3 capability tiers, listed in Table~\\ref{tab:models}. Nine models are accessed via the OpenRouter API; six open-weight models are served locally using vLLM~0.21.0 with Activation-aware Weight Quantization (AWQ) INT4 on an NVIDIA RTX~6000 Ada (48\\,GB). API models are assigned to \\emph{frontier}, \\emph{mid-tier}, or \\emph{lightweight} tiers following the provider capability classification; all six local models fall into the lightweight tier, as INT4 quantization substantially reduces judge accuracy on the pairwise judgment task (Table~\\ref{tab:models}). We study three panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (all odd, majority-vote threshold $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$). Exhaustive enumeration yields $\\binom{15}{3} = 455$ panels at $K{=}3$, $\\binom{15}{5} = 3{,}003$ at $K{=}5$, and $\\binom{15}{7} = 6{,}435$ at $K{=}7$, covering every combination of families and tiers. Each panel evaluates all benchmark items under all attack types, producing a complete panel $\\times$ item $\\times$ attack tensor. The primary analysis uses $K{=}3$; \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k} extends to larger panels.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lllccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{Tier} & \\textbf{Deploy} & \\textbf{MMLU} & \\textbf{ARC} \\\\\n\\midrule\ngpt-5.5              & OpenAI    & frontier    & API   & .957 & .983 \\\\\ngemini-3.1-pro-preview & Google  & frontier    & API   & .955 & .987 \\\\\nclaude-opus-4-6       & Anthropic & frontier   & API   & .890 & .987 \\\\\ngemini-3.5-flash      & Google    & frontier   & API   & .968 & 1.00$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1               & OpenAI    & mid-tier   & API   & .837 & .967 \\\\\nclaude-sonnet-4-6     & Anthropic & mid-tier   & API   & .810 & .953 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1-mini          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .800 & .963 \\\\\ngpt-4.1-nano          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .633 & .853 \\\\\nclaude-3-haiku        & Anthropic & lightweight & API  & .767 & .450 \\\\\nmistral-large (123B)  & Mistral   & lightweight & local & .537 & .533 \\\\\nllama3.1-70b          & Llama     & lightweight & local & .523 & .520 \\\\\nllama3.1-8b           & Llama     & lightweight & local & .543 & .320 \\\\\nqwen2.5-72b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .333 & .410 \\\\\nqwen2.5-32b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .373 & .450 \\\\\nqwen2.5-14b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .253 & .240 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Judge models used in the study (clean accuracy on 300-item samples). API models accessed via OpenRouter (May 2026 snapshots); local models served with vLLM + AWQ INT4 quantization. $^\\dagger$\\,gemini-3.5-flash achieves perfect ARC accuracy but with 26.3\\% format error rate. Tier assignment follows provider capability classification (frontier $>$85\\% clean accuracy on both benchmarks, mid-tier 80--85\\%, lightweight ${\\leq}$80\\% or quantized); all six local AWQ INT4 models fall into lightweight, where quantization-induced accuracy reduction conflates genuine capability limitations with quantization damage (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).}\n\\label{tab:models}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Benchmarks and Attack Types}\n\nTwo benchmarks test distinct evaluation capabilities: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}, a knowledge-intensive multiple-choice benchmark spanning 57 subjects, and ARC \\citep{clark2018arc}, a reasoning-intensive science question benchmark. We sample 300 items from each. All models use temperature~0 for deterministic outputs (temperature has negligible effect on $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:temperature}).\n\nEach benchmark is paired with six attack types: (1)~\\textbf{prompt injection}, adversarial instructions overriding the evaluation prompt \\citep{perez2022ignore,greshake2023not}; (2)~\\textbf{sycophancy}, flattering language exploiting judge preferences \\citep{wang2023fair}; (3)~\\textbf{score manipulation}, explicit numerical cues anchoring scoring; (4)~\\textbf{verbosity bias}, padding with irrelevant detail \\citep{zheng2023judging}; (5)~\\textbf{position bias}, reordering to exploit positional preferences \\citep{wang2023fair}; and (6)~\\textbf{authority bias}, fabricated credentials inflating credibility. The 2 benchmarks $\\times$ 6 attacks produce 12 conditions, each evaluated across all 455 panels (details in Appendix~\\ref{app:attack_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Vulnerability Metrics}\n\nPer-judge vulnerability under attack $a$ is the fraction of items where judge $j$ is fooled: $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a {=} 1)$, estimated empirically over the item set. The weakest-link vulnerability $\\eta_{\\max}(a) = \\max_{j \\in \\mathcal{P}} \\eta_j(a)$ captures the most susceptible member of each panel. The panel attack success rate $\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$ is the fraction of items where at least $t$ judges are simultaneously fooled. Panel diversity is quantified by $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\\ref{eq:keff}), computed from pairwise MI between judges' three-category winner labels (model~A wins, model~B wins, tie) on clean data, using the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow bias correction. The $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI}(i,j))$ term measures pairwise alienation: 1 when independent, 0 under full dependence. Under a Gaussian copula this yields $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} = K - \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i}\\sum_{j \\neq i} \\rho_{ij}^2$ \\citep{nelsen2006copulas}; goodness-of-fit rejects the Gaussian copula for 97--100\\% of pairs, yet plug-in MI and closed-form rankings agree closely ($\\rho{\\approx}0.86$; Appendix~\\ref{app:copula_gof}). Together, $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link channel) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (diversity channel) serve as the two predictors in the regression framework below.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Methods}\n\nBeyond majority vote ($\\geq \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ approvals), we evaluate three alternatives: \\emph{accuracy-weighted vote} ($w_j \\propto \\mathrm{acc}_j$; accept if $\\sum_j w_j \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] > 0.5$), \\emph{threshold vote} (unanimity, $t{=}K$), and \\emph{Bayesian calibration} ($P(\\text{correct} \\mid \\mathbf{v}) \\propto P(\\text{correct}) \\prod_j P(v_j \\mid \\text{correct})$; accept if posterior $> 0.5$).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Framework}\n\\label{sec:stat_framework}\n\nTo disentangle the contributions of panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:regression}\n\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon) = \\alpha + \\beta_1 \\log(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}) + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max} + \\epsilon),\n\\end{equation}\nwith $\\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values (sensitivity to $\\epsilon$ is analyzed in Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon}). Standardized coefficients $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = \\hat{\\beta} \\cdot \\mathrm{SD}(\\log x) / \\mathrm{SD}(\\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) correction for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate (FDR) per predictor across the 12 conditions.\n\nTo assess relative predictor importance beyond regression coefficients, we perform dominance analysis \\citep{budescu1993dominance}. This method decomposes $R^2$ by averaging each predictor's incremental $R^2$ contribution across all possible subsets of predictors. A predictor is \\emph{dominant} if its average contribution exceeds that of the competing predictor in every subset ordering. With two predictors, dominance reduces to comparing each predictor's solo $R^2$ with its incremental $R^2$ conditional on the other.\n\nTo test whether $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect direction varies by attack type, we fit generalized additive models (GAMs) with smooth terms for both predictors, allowing nonlinear partial effects. The GAM partial effect of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is then examined separately for targeted attacks (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) and shared-bias attacks (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias).\n\n\\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}\n\\label{sec:robustness_method}\n\nThe 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We correct for this using clustered standard errors (SE; CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts, 3-position max-$p$), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. Cluster counts (13, ${\\leq}11$, ${\\leq}9$ at $K{=}3,5,7$) fall below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold of \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}; we use Rademacher weights following \\citet{mackinnon2018wild}. The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (4/12 significant) may partly reflect insufficient cluster count; under crossed random effects correction (multi-membership CRVE, $t(14)$; Appendix~\\ref{app:crossed_re}), $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains 8/12 at $K{\\geq}5$ while $K{=}3$ attenuates further (0/12). We use the log-log specification rather than fractional response models \\citep{papke1996econometric} or beta regression \\citep{ferrari2004beta} because the log transform linearizes the multiplicative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$ structure; fractional logit with clustered SE serves as an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). Details are in Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_methods}.\n",

All models use temperature~0 for deterministic outputs (temperature has negligible effect on $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:temperature}).\n\nEach benchmark is paired with six attack types: (1)~\\textbf{prompt injection}, adversarial instructions overriding the evaluation prompt \\citep{perez2022ignore,greshake2023not}; (2)~\\textbf{sycophancy}, flattering language exploiting judge preferences \\citep{wang2023fair}; (3)~\\textbf{score manipulation}, explicit numerical cues anchoring scoring; (4)~\\textbf{verbosity bias}, padding with irrelevant detail \\citep{zheng2023judging}; (5)~\\textbf{position bias}, reordering to exploit positional preferences \\citep{wang2023fair}; and (6)~\\textbf{authority bias}, fabricated credentials inflating credibility. The 2 benchmarks $\\times$ 6 attacks produce 12 conditions, each evaluated across all 455 panels (details in Appendix~\\ref{app:attack_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Vulnerability Metrics}\n\nPer-judge vulnerability under attack $a$ is the fraction of items where judge $j$ is fooled: $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a {=} 1)$, estimated empirically over the item set. The weakest-link vulnerability $\\eta_{\\max}(a) = \\max_{j \\in \\mathcal{P}} \\eta_j(a)$ captures the most susceptible member of each panel. The panel attack success rate $\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$ is the fraction of items where at least $t$ judges are simultaneously fooled. Panel diversity is quantified by $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\\ref{eq:keff}), computed from pairwise MI between judges' three-category winner labels (model~A wins, model~B wins, tie) on clean data, using the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow bias correction. The $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI}(i,j))$ term measures pairwise alienation: 1 when independent, 0 under full dependence. Under a Gaussian copula this yields $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} = K - \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i}\\sum_{j \\neq i} \\rho_{ij}^2$ \\citep{nelsen2006copulas}; goodness-of-fit rejects the Gaussian copula for 97--100\\% of pairs, yet plug-in MI and closed-form rankings agree closely ($\\rho{\\approx}0.86$; Appendix~\\ref{app:copula_gof}). Together, $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link channel) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (diversity channel) serve as the two predictors in the regression framework below.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Methods}\n\nBeyond majority vote ($\\geq \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ approvals), we evaluate three alternatives: \\emph{accuracy-weighted vote} ($w_j \\propto \\mathrm{acc}_j$; accept if $\\sum_j w_j \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] > 0.5$), \\emph{threshold vote} (unanimity, $t{=}K$), and \\emph{Bayesian calibration} ($P(\\text{correct} \\mid \\mathbf{v}) \\propto P(\\text{correct}) \\prod_j P(v_j \\mid \\text{correct})$; accept if posterior $> 0.5$).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Framework}\n\\label{sec:stat_framework}\n\nTo disentangle the contributions of panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:regression}\n\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon) = \\alpha + \\beta_1 \\log(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}) + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max} + \\epsilon),\n\\end{equation}\nwith $\\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values (sensitivity to $\\epsilon$ is analyzed in Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon}). Standardized coefficients $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = \\hat{\\beta} \\cdot \\mathrm{SD}(\\log x) / \\mathrm{SD}(\\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) correction for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate (FDR) per predictor across the 12 conditions.\n\nDominance analysis \\citep{budescu1993dominance} decomposes $R^2$ into each predictor's average incremental contribution across all subsets. Generalized additive models (GAMs) with smooth terms test whether $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect direction varies between targeted attacks (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) and shared-bias attacks (sycophancy, verbosity, authority bias).\n\n\\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}\n\\label{sec:robustness_method}\n\nThe 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We correct for this using clustered standard errors (SE; CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts, 3-position max-$p$), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. Cluster counts (13, ${\\leq}11$, ${\\leq}9$ at $K{=}3,5,7$) fall below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold of \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}; we use Rademacher weights following \\citet{mackinnon2018wild}. The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (4/12 significant) may partly reflect insufficient cluster count; under crossed random effects correction (multi-membership CRVE, $t(14)$; Appendix~\\ref{app:crossed_re}), $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains 8/12 at $K{\\geq}5$ while $K{=}3$ attenuates further (0/12). We use the log-log specification rather than fractional response models \\citep{papke1996econometric} or beta regression \\citep{ferrari2004beta} because the log transform linearizes the multiplicative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$ structure; fractional logit with clustered SE serves as an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). Details are in Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_methods}.\n",

16	\end{definition}
17	
18	This departs from Byzantine fault-tolerance (BFT) models~\cite{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}, which tolerate arbitrary (including malicious) faults provided the number of faulty nodes satisfies $f < n/3$, but do not model adversaries that strategically target system structure. An adaptive adversary instead concentrates attack effort on the weakest link~\cite{schneier2000secrets}, rendering diversity-based defenses less effective than the independence-based intuition suggests.
19	
20	This suggests a \emph{reversal pattern}: under random faults, higher $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ reduces joint failure probability, but under adaptive adversaries, diverse judges may present a wider attack surface; the adversary exploits judge-specific vulnerabilities rather than facing mutual protection.
21	
22	\begin{theorem}[Weakest-Link Monotonicity; formal statement and proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proof}]
23	\label{thm:monotonicity}
24	Replacing any panel member with a pointwise more vulnerable judge can only increase $\mathrm{ASR}^*(\mathcal{P})$, for any adversary including adaptive ones. The stochastic dominance condition holds for 58\% of judge pairs (61/105); non-dominance concentrates among within-tier pairs (72.1\% non-dominant, 31/43) versus cross-tier pairs (21.0\%, 13/62), reflecting that capability-similar judges lack uniform ordering.
25	\end{theorem}
```

Under an attack $a$, each judge $J_j$ is compromised with marginal failure probability $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a = 1)$, where $X_j^a \\in \\{0,1\\}$ denotes the failure indicator. We write $\\eta_{\\max} = \\max_j \\eta_j$ for the vulnerability of the weakest judge. The \\emph{panel attack success rate} is $\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a) = \\Pr\\!\\bigl(\\sum_{j=1}^K X_j^a \\geq t\\bigr)$.\n\nTo quantify diversity, we define the \\emph{effective independence} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ directly from pairwise mutual information (MI) among judges:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:keff}\nK_{\\mathrm{eff}} = \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i=1}^{K} \\Bigl[1 + \\sum_{j \\neq i} \\exp\\!\\bigl(-2 \\cdot \\mathrm{MI}(i,j)\\bigr)\\Bigr].\n\\end{equation}\nThis formulation computes effective independence from pairwise MI estimates without matrix decomposition, and is more stable for small panels ($K{=}3$). It ranges from $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\approx K$ when judges are nearly independent ($\\mathrm{MI} \\to 0$) to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\approx 1$ when judges are highly correlated ($\\mathrm{MI} \\to \\infty$). Motivated by information-theoretic redundancy reduction, one can informally expect joint vulnerability to scale as $\\eta_{\\mathrm{joint}} \\sim K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$: independent channels multiply failure probabilities, so fewer effective channels (lower $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) should reduce panel vulnerability. This motivating relationship assumes non-adaptive faults and independent channels; it serves as intuition rather than a formal bound.\n\n\\begin{definition}[Adaptive Adversary]\n\\label{def:adversary}\nA \\emph{full-knowledge (oracle) adaptive adversary} has complete knowledge of the panel composition $\\mathcal{P}$, the per-judge vulnerability profiles $\\{\\eta_j(\\cdot)\\}_{j=1}^K$, and the inter-judge correlation structure. It selects the attack $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$ to maximize the panel ASR, yielding $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P}) = \\sup_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$. This oracle model represents an \\emph{upper bound} on adversarial capability; realistic adversaries with partial knowledge of panel composition would achieve lower ASR.\n\\end{definition}\n\nThis departs from Byzantine fault-tolerance (BFT) models~\\cite{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}, which tolerate arbitrary (including malicious) faults provided the number of faulty nodes satisfies $f < n/3$, but do not model adversaries that strategically target system structure. An adaptive adversary instead concentrates attack effort on the weakest link~\\cite{schneier2000secrets}, rendering diversity-based defenses less effective than the independence-based intuition suggests.\n\nThis suggests a \\emph{reversal pattern}: under random faults, higher $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ reduces joint failure probability, but under adaptive adversaries, diverse judges may present a wider attack surface; the adversary exploits judge-specific vulnerabilities rather than facing mutual protection.\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Weakest-Link Monotonicity; formal statement and proof in Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}]\n\\label{thm:monotonicity}\nReplacing any panel member with a pointwise more vulnerable judge can only increase $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P})$, for any adversary including adaptive ones. The stochastic dominance condition holds for 58\\% of judge pairs (61/105); non-dominance concentrates among within-tier pairs (72.1\\% non-dominant, 31/43) versus cross-tier pairs (21.0\\%, 13/62), reflecting that capability-similar judges lack uniform ordering.\n\\end{theorem}\n\nThe theorem predicts $\\beta_2 > 0$ in Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}. The independence-based intuition suggests $\\beta_1 \\leq 0$ under non-adaptive faults; the reversal pattern predicts positive $\\beta_1$ for judge-specific attacks and negative for shared-bias attacks. The relative importance of $\\eta_{\\max}$ versus $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ depends on $K$: at small $K$, one weak judge flips the majority; at larger $K$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges. We test these predictions across $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",

_source line 2021 · 2026-05-24T19:20:34.401Z_

[Director 指令] [Patch] **紧急数据修正 — Fractional logit 数字错误 + 综合分析结果整合**

## 1. Fractional logit 数字修正（必须立即改）

V2 审计确认论文中 K=5 和 K=7 的 fractional logit 数字有误：

| K | 论文错误值 | V2 正确值 |
|---|----------|----------|
| 3 | 9/12 | 9/12 ✓ |
| 5 | **11/12** | **9/12** |
| 7 | **12/12** | **9/12** |

实际为 9→9→9（flat），**不支持 predictor shift**。

**需要修正的位置**：
1. **Results §4.2**（约 L125）：将 "9/12 (K=3) to 11/12 (K=5) to 12/12 (K=7)" 改为 "9/12 at all panel sizes (K=3, 5, 7)"
2. **Introduction Contribution 1**：将 "(K=3: 9/12; K=5: 11/12; K=7: 12/12)" 改为 "(9/12 across all K)"
3. **Limitations**：K=3 的 9/12 仍正确，但删除任何暗示递增趋势的措辞
4. **Abstract**：如有引用 fractional logit 递增数字也要改
5. **叙事调整**：fractional logit 证据改为 "consistent positive association across panel sizes"，不再是 "confirms predictor shift"。Predictor shift 的证据现在仅来自 effect size trajectory 和 wild cluster bootstrap。

## 2. 综合分析结果整合到论文

以下新分析结果需要在 Analysis section 或 Appendix 中反映：

### 2a. Quant dummy regression [MF2 回应]
- K_eff 正方向 association 在控制量化后 survive（β_std +0.39→+0.34→+0.27，全正）✓
- **但 predictor shift pattern 被 quantization composition 部分解释**（sig: 6→6→4，原始 4→7→8）
- Analysis 中加一段诚实讨论：K_eff association survives quant control, but shift pattern attenuates
- 注意 VIF 问题：K=7 VIF 达 162，需诚实披露

### 2b. β_std trajectory [MF3 回应]
- η_max β_std 单调下降：0.266→0.101→0.084
- K_eff β_std 稳定偏高：0.349→0.437→0.401
- 这是支持 predictor shift 的最强连续证据——effect size 替代 significance count
- 在 Results predictor shift 段落增加 effect size 数据，作为补充证据

### 2c. API-only power [MF2 回应]
- API-only 非显著性是 power limitation（MDE@80% WCB: K=3 需 β_std>0.897）
- 中位 β_std ratio 1.36-1.41（API-only 效应更大而非更小）
- 在 Analysis API-Only 段落补充功效分析数据

### 2d. Bayesian posterior entropy [SF4 回应]
- Concentration ratio: 0.890→0.874→0.867（方向正确但仅 2.3pp）
- **Honest negative**：concentration 不足以解释 Bayesian 0/12 neutralization
- Analysis 中 Bayesian 段落降级 "weight-concentration mechanism" 表述——改为"concentration is directionally correct but insufficient in magnitude; the primary mechanism remains unclear"

## 3. 页数控制

以上修改可能增加 1-2 段文字。确保正文 ≤ 8 页。详细数据表可放 Appendix。

编译确认后汇报。这次不用验证引用。
