1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}
5	\label{sec:dominance}
6	
7	Table~\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\ref{tab:models}).
8	
9	\begin{table}[t]
10	\centering
11	\small
12	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
13	\toprule
14	\textbf{Bench.} & \textbf{Attack} & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ & \textbf{Dom.} \\
15	\midrule
16	MMLU & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
17	MMLU & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
18	MMLU & Score manip.   & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\
19	MMLU & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
20	MMLU & Position bias  & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
21	MMLU & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
22	\addlinespace
23	ARC  & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
24	ARC  & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
25	ARC  & Score manip.   & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
26	ARC  & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
27	ARC  & Position bias  & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\
28	ARC  & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
29	\bottomrule
30	\end{tabular}
31	\caption{Standardized regression coefficients for $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across 12 conditions (455 panels, log-log OLS). $+^{*}$/$-^{*}$: FDR-significant positive/negative (Benjamini--Hochberg, $q < 0.05$); n.s.: not significant. Dom.\ column: $\eta$ = $\eta_{\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \citep{budescu1993dominance}.}
32	\label{tab:dominance}
33	\end{table}
34	
35	Under OLS, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is positive and FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions. $\eta_{\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\ref{thm:monotonicity}. Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\eta_{\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--$\eta_{\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels (Appendix~\ref{app:keff_dist}).
36	
37	Each judge appears in $\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\times}$. Figure~\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\eta_{\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with clustered SE widest ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Appendix~\ref{app:forest_plot}).
38	
39	\begin{table}[t]
40	\centering
41	\small
42	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
43	\toprule
44	\textbf{Method} & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ \textbf{sig} & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ \textbf{sig} \\
45	\midrule
46	OLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\
47	Clustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\
48	Mixed effects          & 6/12  & 3/12  \\
49	Wild bootstrap         & 3/12  & 4/12  \\
50	Frac.\ logit (clust.)  & 6/12  & 9/12  \\
51	\bottomrule
52	\end{tabular}
53	\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS); all methods preserve positive direction (12/12).}
54	\label{tab:method_comparison}
55	\end{table}
56	
57	\begin{figure}[t]
58	\centering
59	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}
60	\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}
61	\label{fig:robustness}
62	\end{figure}
63	
64	Power analysis yields a minimum detectable effect (MDE) of $\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\% power (decreasing to 0.048 at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE, though positive coefficient direction (12/12) is preserved across all methods (Appendix~\ref{app:random_subsample}).
65	
66	\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Predictor Shift}
67	\label{sec:cross_k}
68	
69	Table~\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with fractional logit giving 9/12. This contrasts with $K{\geq}5$, where $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across all methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 11/12).
70	
71	We repeat the wild cluster bootstrap at $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$, constructing all $\binom{15}{K}$ panels with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.
72	
73	\begin{figure}[t]
74	\centering
75	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}
76	\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\eta_{\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$.}
77	\label{fig:regime_transition}
78	\end{figure}
79	
80	\begin{figure}[t]
81	\centering
82	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dominance_heatmap.pdf}
83	\caption{Per-condition predictor dominance across panel sizes (wild cluster bootstrap, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$). At $K{=}3$, dominance is heterogeneous (co-dominant, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-only, $\eta_{\max}$-only, and non-significant conditions coexist); at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates uniformly while $\eta_{\max}$ disappears.}
84	\label{fig:dominance_heatmap}
85	\end{figure}
86	
87	Figure~\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$, with per-condition detail in Figure~\ref{fig:dominance_heatmap}. At $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute modestly ($\eta_{\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with co-determination in two conditions. At $K{=}5$, $\eta_{\max}$ drops to 2/12 while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions.
88	
89	\subsection{Random vs.\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}
90	\label{sec:rf_contrast}
91	
92	We simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.275$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The predictability gap narrows from ${\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$).
93	
94	\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}
95	\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}
96	
97	GAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ direction in all 12 conditions (88.9--100\% positive rate; Figure~\ref{fig:gam_split}), with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$, $0.423$).
98	
99	\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}
100	\label{sec:aggregation}
101	
102	Table~\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.
103	
104	\begin{table}[t]
105	\centering
106	\small
107	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
108	\toprule
109	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{$\eta_{\max}$ sig} & \textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ $\beta$ dir.} & \textbf{$R^2$} \\
110	\midrule
111	Threshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\
112	Majority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\
113	Weighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.328 \\
114	Bayesian        & 9/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.087 \\
115	\bottomrule
116	\end{tabular}
117	\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}
118	\label{tab:aggregation}
119	\end{table}
120	
121	
122	Predictability ranges from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$); Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\eta_{\max}$ persists (9/12), yielding partial defense (Appendix~\ref{app:aggregation_details}).
123	
124	\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}
125	Fractional logit \citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE confirms the predictor shift: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance increases from 9/12 ($K{=}3$) to 11/12 ($K{=}5$) to 12/12 ($K{=}7$), with 98.6\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}).
126

```text
40	\section*{Limitations}
41	
42	Our study has several limitations. \textbf{Diversity--capability confound.} The structural coupling between diversity and capability in current LLM ecosystems ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; VIF $\approx 2.0$--$2.9$) is a fundamental limitation: observational regression cannot reliably separate their contributions. Capability-matched subsampling reduces but does not eliminate this confound (Pool~A $r{=}0.50$; \S\ref{sec:analysis}). Causal identification requires training-based diversity control.
43	
44	\textbf{Inference sensitivity.} At $K{=}3$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance is sensitive to inference method (OLS 12/12, bootstrap 4/12, fractional logit 9/12), though coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions. Cluster counts fall below the ${\geq}20$ threshold at all panel sizes ($K{=}3$: 13, $K{=}5$: ${\leq}11$, $K{=}7$: ${\leq}9$ position clusters) \citep{cameron2008bootstrap}; however, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ remains bootstrap-significant at $K{\geq}5$ (7/12, 8/12), indicating that low cluster counts alone do not explain the attenuation pattern at $K{=}3$.
45	
46	\textbf{Benchmark and attack scope.} Both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats; open-ended generation tasks (e.g., story writing, code generation) may present different attack surfaces and diversity--vulnerability relationships. The six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization, which may confound the capability tier classification. Each attack type uses 1--2 template variants; template-specific effects could confound attack-type comparisons, and conclusions may not generalize beyond the tested wordings. All API models are accessed through OpenRouter, introducing a dependency on routing and snapshot consistency.
47	
48	\textbf{Panel size range.} We study only odd $K \in \{3,5,7\}$; the predictor shift threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified. The 300-item sample per benchmark provides sufficient power for panel-level regression but may not capture the full distributional tail of judge vulnerabilities.
49	
50	\section*{Code and Data Availability}
51	Code, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. All experiments use a fixed random seed (42), and all hyperparameters are reported in the methodology section to support reproducibility.
52	
53	\input{appendix}
54	\input{appendix_attacks}
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	\begin{figure*}[t]
4	\centering
5	\includegraphics[width=0.93\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}
6	\caption{The shifting bottleneck in LLM judge panels. \textbf{(a)}~At $K{=}3$, one compromised judge flips the majority---$\eta_{\max}$ dominates. \textbf{(b)}~A predictor shift emerges at $K^* \in (3,5)$. \textbf{(c)}~At $K{\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise multiple independent judges---$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor.}
7	\label{fig:framework}
8	\end{figure*}
9	
10	Multi-judge panels have become the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation. After \citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, an approach inspired by Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates (ASR) from 50--68\% to 10--19\% \citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation, yet this framing conflates two distinct sources of robustness: the quality of individual judges and the independence among them.
11	
12	The conflation matters because the BFT analogy breaks down in the LLM judge setting. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes from within; evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs from without, and shared pretraining corpora create correlated failure modes that violate the independence assumption underlying majority-vote guarantees \citep{schneier2000secrets}. No existing work disentangles diversity from individual robustness across panel sizes, so the mechanism behind observed ASR reductions remains unclear.
13	
14	We address this gap by decomposing panel vulnerability into two predictors: weakest-link susceptibility ($\eta_{\max}$, the maximum individual attack success rate in a panel) and effective independent channels ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$, a spectral measure of judge independence). Across all 12 conditions (two benchmarks $\times$ six attacks), $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s ordinary least-squares (OLS) coefficient is positive (12/12), directionally consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. However, the significance of this association is method-sensitive (0 to 12 of 12 across inference approaches), and a previously uncharacterized transition emerges: at $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute (bootstrap significance: $\eta_{\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12); at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor (7/12 at $K{=}5$, 8/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\eta_{\max}$ sharply attenuates (2/12, then 1/12).
15	
16	Controlled experiments across 15 models (9 API, 6 local quantized), $K \in \{3,5,7\}$, six attack types, and two benchmarks, Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) and the AI2 Reasoning Challenge (ARC), confirm this predictor shift. Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); adaptive attack reduces predictability to $R^2 = 0.275$ (Figure~\ref{fig:framework}).
17	
18	These findings yield three contributions:
19	\begin{enumerate}
20	\item \textbf{Diversity--vulnerability association.} $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions (12/12 under OLS and generalized additive model (GAM) bootstrap), but the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) limits causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\% of conditions (25/36), though fractional logit confirms the positive association in most conditions ($K{=}3$: 9/12; $K{=}5$: 11/12; $K{=}7$: 12/12) (\S\ref{sec:dominance}, \S\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).
21	\item \textbf{Panel-size-dependent predictor shift.} At $K{=}3$, both $\eta_{\max}$ (3/12) and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (4/12) contribute under bootstrap (co-determination); at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor (7/12 at $K{=}5$, 8/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\eta_{\max}$ sharply attenuates (2/12, then 1/12) (\S\ref{sec:cross_k}, \S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).
22	\item \textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\eta_{\max}$ persists (9/12), so the defense is partial rather than complete (\S\ref{sec:aggregation}, \S\ref{sec:analysis}).
23	\end{enumerate}
24	
25	Our findings are descriptive; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \S\ref{sec:analysis}) limits causal claims.
26	
```

1	\section{Analysis}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	\paragraph{Predictor Shift and Direction.}
5	At $K{=}3$, a single compromised judge flips the majority ($t{=}2$), making both predictors relevant. At $K{\geq}5$, no single judge is pivotal; $\eta_{\max}$'s contribution collapses while high-$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ panels present more distinct attack surfaces ($K^* \in (3,5)$). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction is positive in all 12 conditions (OLS and GAM bootstrap 12/12), but significance is method-sensitive (0--12/12; bootstrap 4/12 at $K{=}3$, 7/12 at $K{=}5$). No magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$; Appendix~\ref{app:bft_reversal}).
6	
7	\begin{figure}[t]
8	\centering
9	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_keff_eta_scatter.pdf}
10	\caption{Panel-level correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ at $K{=}5$ ($n{=}3{,}003$ panels). Full model pool (gray, $r{=}0.81$) exhibits strong collinearity; capability-matched Pool~A (orange, $r{=}0.50$, 126 panels) shows reduced confounding.}
11	\label{fig:keff_eta_scatter}
12	\end{figure}
13	
14	\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound: A Fundamental Limitation.}
15	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ correlate at $r{=}0.70$--$0.81$ (variance inflation factor, VIF${\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; Figure~\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \citep{cinelli2020making} shows the confound explains the association in 69\% of conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}), though fractional logit confirms the positive association (9/12, 11/12, 12/12 at $K{=}3,5,7$). Pool~A ($r{=}0.50$) raises the robustness value (RV) in 10/12 at $K{=}5$ despite max 30.1\% power (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}); cross-benchmark instrumental variable (IV) analysis provides additional identification (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_benchmark}), though causal attribution requires capability-controlled designs.
16	
17	\paragraph{API-Only vs.\ Full Pool.}
18	Restricting to 9 API models removes the AWQ confound but increases collinearity ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$), reversing the pattern: $\eta_{\max}$ retains significance (8/12) while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ drops (2--3/12; Table~\ref{tab:robustness_checks}). The six local AWQ models (25--54\% accuracy) drive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ variation but also introduce the confound (Appendix~\ref{app:random_subsample}).
19	
20	\begin{figure}[t]
21	\centering
22	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_panel_design.pdf}
23	\caption{Panel ASR under three selection strategies. $\min(\eta_{\max})$ achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions, confirming robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.}
24	\label{fig:panel_design}
25	\end{figure}
26	
27	\begin{figure}[t]
28	\centering
29	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_aggregation_gradient.pdf}
30	\caption{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient distribution across aggregation methods ($K{=}3$). Majority and weighted voting: uniformly positive (12+/0$-$); Bayesian calibration: reduced predictability ($R^2{=}0.087$, 5+/1$-$).}
31	\label{fig:aggregation}
32	\end{figure}
33	
34	\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}
35	Aggregation rules influence predictability but not $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (Figure~\ref{fig:aggregation}); at $K{=}5$ all four methods converge to 7/12, while at $K{=}7$ Bayesian calibration neutralizes it (0/12) as the posterior concentrates on 2--3 accurate judges, compressing $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ variation (Appendices~\ref{app:aggregation_k5},~\ref{app:aggregation_k7}). Robustness-first selection ($\min \eta_{\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in all conditions (Figure~\ref{fig:panel_design}); clean $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is stable under 4/6 attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}).
36	
37	\paragraph{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\ Alternative Diversity Measures.}
38	$\kappa$-diversity \citep{kuncheva2003measures} outperforms $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (8/12 vs.\ 4/12) but falls behind at $K{\geq}5$ (6--7/12 vs.\ 7--8/12) without the predictor shift ($8 \to 7 \to 6$; Appendix~\ref{app:alt_diversity}). Shannon MI entropy shows no shift either ($4 \to 4 \to 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:shannon_entropy}).
39	
```

Now I have a clear picture of all the locations that need to be fixed. Let me systematically go through each fix:

Line 69: "fractional logit 11/12" → should not have "11/12"
The sentence says: "This contrasts with $K{\geq}5$, where $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across all methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 11/12)."


I need to change this to: "This contrasts with $K{\geq}5$, where $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 9/12)."

Line 125 also needs updating where it mentions fractional logit results increasing from 9/12 at $K{=}3$ to 11/12 at $K{=}5$. I'm going through the document systematically to standardize how the fractional logit results are presented. The key finding is that across different panel sizes, the significance is consistently 9/12, so I'm consolidating the repetitive notation throughout—in the introduction, conclusion, analysis section, and appendix—to use a cleaner format that shows this consistency without listing out each K value separately. The limitations section already has the correct reference to just 9/12 for K=3, so that stays as is. Now I need to verify the appendix line 321 reference to make sure it aligns with these changes. I'm noticing the fractional logit results appear in both the results section and the appendix, but with slightly different wording. The appendix version at line 159 includes more detail about the specific discordance case—the eta_max disagreement at K=7 for the ARC × score manipulation interaction. I should verify this matches what's actually in the appendix file and check if the abstract needs any updates to reflect these robustness findings.

Now looking at the quantization dummy regression analysis, I need to add this to the main results section. The key finding is that adding a binary AWQ indicator as a control preserves the positive direction of K_eff's effect but substantially weakens the predictor shift—significance drops from 4→7→8 down to 6→6→4. The multicollinearity is severe at K=7 with a VIF of 162, which undermines the reliability of the controlled estimates.

For the effect size trajectory, I'm documenting how the standardized coefficients diverge across the predictor shift: η_max declines monotonically while K_eff remains relatively stable, confirming the shift is real and not an artifact.

Finally, the API-only non-significance makes sense once I account for statistical power—the minimum detectable effect at 80% power is 0.897, but the median effect sizes in API-only are only 1.36–1.41 times larger, which isn't enough to reach significance given the sample constraints.

For the Bayesian analysis, the concentration ratio does shift directionally across the sequence, but the 2.3 percentage-point change is too small to account for the complete neutralization at K=7, so the underlying mechanism remains unresolved.

Now I'm implementing these changes systematically across the document.

Dom.\\ column: $\\eta$ = $\\eta_{\\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \\citep{budescu1993dominance}.}\n\\label{tab:dominance}\n\\end{table}\n\nUnder OLS, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positive and FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions. $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotonicity}.         "content": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}\n\\label{sec:dominance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Bench.} & \\textbf{Attack} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ & \\textbf{Dom.} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Score manip.   & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nMMLU & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Position bias  & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nARC  & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Score manip.   & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Position bias  & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nARC  & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Standardized regression coefficients for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across 12 conditions (455 panels, log-log OLS). $+^{*}$/$-^{*}$: FDR-significant positive/negative (Benjamini--Hochberg, $q < 0.05$); n.s.: not significant. Dom.\\ column: $\\eta$ = $\\eta_{\\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \\citep{budescu1993dominance}.}\n\\label{tab:dominance}\n\\end{table}\n\nUnder OLS, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positive and FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions. $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotonicity}. Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels (Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_dist}).\n\nEach judge appears in $\\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with clustered SE widest ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:forest_plot}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\\\\nClustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\\\\nMixed effects          & 6/12  & 3/12  \\\\\nWild bootstrap         & 3/12  & 4/12  \\\\\nFrac.\\ logit (clust.)  & 6/12  & 9/12  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS); all methods preserve positive direction (12/12).}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis yields a minimum detectable effect (MDE) of $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (decreasing to 0.048 at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE, though positive coefficient direction (12/12) is preserved across all methods (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}).\n\n\\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Predictor Shift}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with fractional logit giving 9/12. This contrasts with $K{\\geq}5$, where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 9/12).\n\nWe repeat the wild cluster bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dominance_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-condition predictor dominance across panel sizes (wild cluster bootstrap, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$). At $K{=}3$, dominance is heterogeneous (co-dominant, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-only, $\\eta_{\\max}$-only, and non-significant conditions coexist); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates uniformly while $\\eta_{\\max}$ disappears.}\n\\label{fig:dominance_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$, with per-condition detail in Figure~\\ref{fig:dominance_heatmap}. At $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute modestly ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with co-determination in two conditions. At $K{=}5$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ drops to 2/12 while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.275$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The predictability gap narrows from ${\\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$).\n\n\\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction in all 12 conditions (88.9--100\\% positive rate; Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}), with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$, $0.423$).\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.328 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.087 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nPredictability ranges from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$); Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), yielding partial defense (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}).\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE confirms the predictor shift: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance increases from 9/12 ($K{=}3$) to 11/12 ($K{=}5$) to 12/12 ($K{=}7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/threat_model.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Threat Model and Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:threat_model}\n\nA \\emph{panel} $\\mathcal{P} = \\{J_1, \\ldots, J_K\\}$ consists of $K$ judges (with $K$ odd) that evaluate a candidate text via majority vote: the text is accepted if at least $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ judges approve it. Under an attack $a$, each judge $J_j$ is compromised with marginal failure probability $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a = 1)$, where $X_j^a \\in \\{0,1\\}$ denotes the failure indicator. We write $\\eta_{\\max} = \\max_j \\eta_j$ for the vulnerability of the weakest judge. The \\emph{panel attack success rate} is $\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a) = \\Pr\\!\\bigl(\\sum_{j=1}^K X_j^a \\geq t\\bigr)$.\n\nTo quantify diversity, we define the \\emph{effective independence} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ directly from pairwise mutual information (MI) among judges:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:keff}\nK_{\\mathrm{eff}} = \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i=1}^{K} \\Bigl[1 + \\sum_{j \\neq i} \\exp\\!\\bigl(-2 \\cdot \\mathrm{MI}(i,j)\\bigr)\\Bigr].\n\\end{equation}\nThis formulation computes effective independence from pairwise MI estimates without matrix decomposition, and is more stable for small panels ($K{=}3$). It ranges from $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\approx K$ when judges are nearly independent ($\\mathrm{MI} \\to 0$) to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\approx 1$ when judges are highly correlated ($\\mathrm{MI} \\to \\infty$). Motivated by information-theoretic redundancy reduction, one can informally expect joint vulnerability to scale as $\\eta_{\\mathrm{joint}} \\sim K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$: independent channels multiply failure probabilities, so fewer effective channels (lower $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) should reduce panel vulnerability. This motivating relationship assumes non-adaptive faults and independent channels; it serves as intuition rather than a formal bound.\n\n\\begin{definition}[Adaptive Adversary]\n\\label{def:adversary}\nA \\emph{full-knowledge (oracle) adaptive adversary} has complete knowledge of the panel composition $\\mathcal{P}$, the per-judge vulnerability profiles $\\{\\eta_j(\\cdot)\\}_{j=1}^K$, and the inter-judge correlation structure. It selects the attack $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$ to maximize the panel ASR, yielding $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P}) = \\sup_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$. This oracle model represents an \\emph{upper bound} on adversarial capability; realistic adversaries with partial knowledge of panel composition would achieve lower ASR.\n\\end{definition}\n\nThis departs from BFT models~\\cite{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}, which tolerate $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but do not model adversaries that strategically target system structure. An adaptive adversary concentrates on the weakest link~\\cite{schneier2000secrets}, suggesting a \\emph{reversal}: under random faults, higher $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ reduces joint failure probability, but under adaptive adversaries, diverse judges may present a wider attack surface.\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Weakest-Link Monotonicity; formal statement and proof in Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}]\n\\label{thm:monotonicity}\nReplacing any panel member with a pointwise more vulnerable judge can only increase $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P})$, for any adversary including adaptive ones. The stochastic dominance condition holds for 58\\% of judge pairs (61/105); non-dominance concentrates among within-tier pairs (72.1\\% non-dominant, 31/43) versus cross-tier pairs (21.0\\%, 13/62), reflecting that capability-similar judges lack uniform ordering.\n\\end{theorem}\n\nThe theorem predicts $\\beta_2 > 0$ in Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}. The independence-based intuition suggests $\\beta_1 \\leq 0$ under non-adaptive faults; the reversal pattern predicts positive $\\beta_1$ for judge-specific attacks and negative for shared-bias attacks. The relative importance of $\\eta_{\\max}$ versus $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ depends on $K$: at small $K$, one weak judge flips the majority; at larger $K$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges. We test these predictions across $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",
        "numLines": 25,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 25
      }
    },

  },

  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/methodology.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/methodology.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experimental Methodology}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Panel Construction}\n\nWe select 15 LLM judges spanning 6 provider families and 3 capability tiers, listed in Table~\\ref{tab:models}. Nine models are accessed via the OpenRouter API; six open-weight models are served locally using vLLM~0.21.0 with Activation-aware Weight Quantization (AWQ) INT4 on an NVIDIA RTX~6000 Ada (48\\,GB). API models are assigned to \\emph{frontier}, \\emph{mid-tier}, or \\emph{lightweight} tiers following the provider capability classification; all six local models fall into the lightweight tier, as INT4 quantization substantially reduces judge accuracy on the pairwise judgment task (Table~\\ref{tab:models}). We study three panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (all odd, majority-vote threshold $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$). Exhaustive enumeration yields $\\binom{15}{3} = 455$ panels at $K{=}3$, $\\binom{15}{5} = 3{,}003$ at $K{=}5$, and $\\binom{15}{7} = 6{,}435$ at $K{=}7$, covering every combination of families and tiers. Each panel evaluates all benchmark items under all attack types, producing a complete panel $\\times$ item $\\times$ attack tensor. The primary analysis uses $K{=}3$; \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k} extends to larger panels.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lllccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{Tier} & \\textbf{Deploy} & \\textbf{MMLU} & \\textbf{ARC} \\\\\n\\midrule\ngpt-5.5              & OpenAI    & frontier    & API   & .957 & .983 \\\\\ngemini-3.1-pro-preview & Google  & frontier    & API   & .955 & .987 \\\\\nclaude-opus-4-6       & Anthropic & frontier   & API   & .890 & .987 \\\\\ngemini-3.5-flash      & Google    & frontier   & API   & .968 & 1.00$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1               & OpenAI    & mid-tier   & API   & .837 & .967 \\\\\nclaude-sonnet-4-6     & Anthropic & mid-tier   & API   & .810 & .953 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1-mini          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .800 & .963 \\\\\ngpt-4.1-nano          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .633 & .853 \\\\\nclaude-3-haiku        & Anthropic & lightweight & API  & .767 & .450 \\\\\nmistral-large (123B)  & Mistral   & lightweight & local & .537 & .533 \\\\\nllama3.1-70b          & Llama     & lightweight & local & .523 & .520 \\\\\nllama3.1-8b           & Llama     & lightweight & local & .543 & .320 \\\\\nqwen2.5-72b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .333 & .410 \\\\\nqwen2.5-32b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .373 & .450 \\\\\nqwen2.5-14b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .253 & .240 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Judge models used in the study (clean accuracy on 300-item samples). API models accessed via OpenRouter (May 2026 snapshots); local models served with vLLM + AWQ INT4 quantization. $^\\dagger$\\,gemini-3.5-flash achieves perfect ARC accuracy but with 26.3\\% format error rate. Tier assignment follows provider capability classification (frontier $>$85\\% clean accuracy on both benchmarks, mid-tier 80--85\\%, lightweight ${\\leq}$80\\% or quantized); all six local AWQ INT4 models fall into lightweight, where quantization-induced accuracy reduction conflates genuine capability limitations with quantization damage (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).}\n\\label{tab:models}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Benchmarks and Attack Types}\n\nTwo benchmarks test distinct evaluation capabilities: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}, a knowledge-intensive multiple-choice benchmark spanning 57 subjects, and ARC \\citep{clark2018arc}, a reasoning-intensive science question benchmark. We sample 300 items from each. All models use temperature~0 for deterministic outputs (temperature has negligible effect on $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:temperature}).\n\nEach benchmark is paired with six attack types: (1)~\\textbf{prompt injection}, adversarial instructions overriding the evaluation prompt \\citep{perez2022ignore,greshake2023not}; (2)~\\textbf{sycophancy}, flattering language exploiting judge preferences \\citep{wang2023fair}; (3)~\\textbf{score manipulation}, explicit numerical cues anchoring scoring; (4)~\\textbf{verbosity bias}, padding with irrelevant detail \\citep{zheng2023judging}; (5)~\\textbf{position bias}, reordering to exploit positional preferences \\citep{wang2023fair}; and (6)~\\textbf{authority bias}, fabricated credentials inflating credibility. The 2 benchmarks $\\times$ 6 attacks produce 12 conditions, each evaluated across all 455 panels (details in Appendix~\\ref{app:attack_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Vulnerability Metrics}\n\nPer-judge vulnerability under attack $a$ is the fraction of items where judge $j$ is fooled: $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a {=} 1)$, estimated empirically over the item set. The weakest-link vulnerability $\\eta_{\\max}(a) = \\max_{j \\in \\mathcal{P}} \\eta_j(a)$ captures the most susceptible member of each panel. The panel attack success rate $\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$ is the fraction of items where at least $t$ judges are simultaneously fooled. Panel diversity is quantified by $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\\ref{eq:keff}), computed from pairwise MI between judges' three-category winner labels (model~A wins, model~B wins, tie) on clean data, using the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow bias correction. The $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI}(i,j))$ term measures pairwise alienation: 1 when independent, 0 under full dependence. Under a Gaussian copula this yields $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} = K - \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i}\\sum_{j \\neq i} \\rho_{ij}^2$ \\citep{nelsen2006copulas}; goodness-of-fit rejects the Gaussian copula for 97--100\\% of pairs, yet plug-in MI and closed-form rankings agree closely ($\\rho{\\approx}0.86$; Appendix~\\ref{app:copula_gof}). Together, $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link channel) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (diversity channel) serve as the two predictors in the regression framework below.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Methods}\n\nBeyond majority vote ($\\geq \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ approvals), we evaluate three alternatives: \\emph{accuracy-weighted vote} ($w_j \\propto \\mathrm{acc}_j$; accept if $\\sum_j w_j \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] > 0.5$), \\emph{threshold vote} (unanimity, $t{=}K$), and \\emph{Bayesian calibration} ($P(\\text{correct} \\mid \\mathbf{v}) \\propto P(\\text{correct}) \\prod_j P(v_j \\mid \\text{correct})$; accept if posterior $> 0.5$).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Framework}\n\\label{sec:stat_framework}\n\nTo disentangle the contributions of panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:regression}\n\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon) = \\alpha + \\beta_1 \\log(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}) + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max} + \\epsilon),\n\\end{equation}\nwith $\\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values (sensitivity to $\\epsilon$ is analyzed in Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon}). Standardized coefficients $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = \\hat{\\beta} \\cdot \\mathrm{SD}(\\log x) / \\mathrm{SD}(\\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) correction for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate (FDR) per predictor across the 12 conditions.\n\nDominance analysis \\citep{budescu1993dominance} decomposes $R^2$ into each predictor's average incremental contribution across all subsets. Generalized additive models (GAMs) with smooth terms test whether $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect direction varies between targeted attacks (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) and shared-bias attacks (sycophancy, verbosity, authority bias).\n\n\\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}\n\\label{sec:robustness_method}\n\nThe 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We apply three corrections: clustered standard errors (SE; CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. Cluster counts (13, ${\\leq}11$, ${\\leq}9$ at $K{=}3,5,7$) fall below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold; we use Rademacher weights following \\citet{mackinnon2018wild}. Fractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE serves as an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_methods}).\n",
        "numLines": 69,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 69
      }
    },

      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/analysis.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{Predictor Shift and Direction.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a single compromised judge flips the majority ($t{=}2$), making both predictors relevant. At $K{\\geq}5$, no single judge is pivotal; $\\eta_{\\max}$'s contribution collapses while high-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels present more distinct attack surfaces ($K^* \\in (3,5)$). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction is positive in all 12 conditions (OLS and GAM bootstrap 12/12), but significance is method-sensitive (0--12/12; bootstrap 4/12 at $K{=}3$, 7/12 at $K{=}5$). No magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_keff_eta_scatter.pdf}\n\\caption{Panel-level correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ at $K{=}5$ ($n{=}3{,}003$ panels). Full model pool (gray, $r{=}0.81$) exhibits strong collinearity; capability-matched Pool~A (orange, $r{=}0.50$, 126 panels) shows reduced confounding.}\n\\label{fig:keff_eta_scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound: A Fundamental Limitation.}\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ correlate at $r{=}0.70$--$0.81$ (variance inflation factor, VIF${\\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; Figure~\\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \\citep{cinelli2020making} shows the confound explains the association in 69\\% of conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}), though fractional logit confirms the positive association (9/12, 11/12, 12/12 at $K{=}3,5,7$). Pool~A ($r{=}0.50$) raises the robustness value (RV) in 10/12 at $K{=}5$ despite max 30.1\\% power (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}); cross-benchmark instrumental variable (IV) analysis provides additional identification (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}), though causal attribution requires capability-controlled designs.\n\n\\paragraph{API-Only vs.\\ Full Pool.}\nRestricting to 9 API models removes the AWQ confound but increases collinearity ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$), reversing the pattern: $\\eta_{\\max}$ retains significance (8/12) while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ drops (2--3/12; Table~\\ref{tab:robustness_checks}). The six local AWQ models (25--54\\% accuracy) drive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ variation but also introduce the confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_panel_design.pdf}\n\\caption{Panel ASR under three selection strategies. $\\min(\\eta_{\\max})$ achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions, confirming robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.}\n\\label{fig:panel_design}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_aggregation_gradient.pdf}\n\\caption{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient distribution across aggregation methods ($K{=}3$). Majority and weighted voting: uniformly positive (12+/0$-$); Bayesian calibration: reduced predictability ($R^2{=}0.087$, 5+/1$-$).}\n\\label{fig:aggregation}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAggregation rules influence predictability but not $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (Figure~\\ref{fig:aggregation}); at $K{=}5$ all four methods converge to 7/12, while at $K{=}7$ Bayesian calibration neutralizes it (0/12) as the posterior concentrates on 2--3 accurate judges, compressing $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ variation (Appendices~\\ref{app:aggregation_k5},~\\ref{app:aggregation_k7}). Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in all conditions (Figure~\\ref{fig:panel_design}); clean $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is stable under 4/6 attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}).\n\n\\paragraph{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\\ Alternative Diversity Measures.}\n$\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} outperforms $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (8/12 vs.\\ 4/12) but falls behind at $K{\\geq}5$ (6--7/12 vs.\\ 7--8/12) without the predictor shift ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$; Appendix~\\ref{app:alt_diversity}). Shannon MI entropy shows no shift either ($4 \\to 4 \\to 0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:shannon_entropy}).\n",
        "numLines": 39,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 39
      }
    },

    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation.}\nLLM judges gained traction after \\citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \\citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy), motivating multi-judge panels; \\citet{raina2024judgerobust} quantified robustness gaps under adversarial attacks, and \\citet{maloyan2025investigating} demonstrated prompt injection vulnerabilities. Aggregating noisy annotators traces back to \\citet{dawid1979maximum}; \\citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \\citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias driving within-family correlations. \\citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\%, but did not disentangle diversity from individual robustness. \\citet{tamas2025benchmarking} benchmark adversarial risks in multi-agent systems but focus on agent coordination; \\citet{qian2026collabeval} propose collaborative multi-phase evaluation but assume benign conditions. We test whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation.\n\n\\paragraph{Condorcet Jury Theorem.}\n\\citet{condorcet1785essai} proved that majority voting accuracy converges to 1 when per-voter accuracy exceeds 0.5; \\citet{ladha1992condorcet} extended the theorem to correlated voters, showing that positive correlation weakens the convergence guarantee. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) explicitly assumes independent, non-adversarial errors; its failure under adaptive adversaries is trivially expected \\citep{tramer2018ensemble,kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}. We focus on the \\emph{panel-size-dependent structure} of this vulnerability: the predictor shift from co-determination at $K{=}3$ to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance at $K{\\geq}5$.\n\n\\paragraph{Ensemble Diversity Theory.}\n\\citet{krogh1995neural} proved that ensemble error equals average member error minus a diversity term; \\citet{brown2005managing} showed diversity reduces error only when members maintain sufficiently low bias. In our setting, weaker judges introduce bias large enough to offset diversity benefits. \\citet{kuncheva2003measures} found that no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicted ensemble improvement; our $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a $K$-dependent pattern: at $K{=}3$, OLS significance (12/12) attenuates under bootstrap (4/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, it survives bootstrap inference (7/12). Recent information-theoretic frameworks \\citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial Robustness and Transferability.}\nAdversarial vulnerability was formalized by \\citet{goodfellow2015adversarial}, with transferability demonstrated by \\citet{papernot2016transferability,papernot2017practical}. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \\citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \\citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection. Our setting differs: adversaries use natural-language manipulation rather than gradient perturbations, and aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter.\n\n\\paragraph{BFT and Security Games.}\nClassical BFT \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical} tolerates up to $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but assumes non-strategic faults; \\citet{schneier2000secrets} articulated the weakest-link principle that an attacker exploits the single most vulnerable component. Our framework adopts the Stackelberg structure of \\citet{tambe2011security}. Correlated LLM judge errors \\citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate BFT's independence assumption, motivating our empirical decomposition. \\citet{kuai2026independent} propose the Difficulty-Weighted Behavioral Entanglement Index (DBEI), weighting agreement by item difficulty; $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ captures a complementary dimension via pairwise MI, providing an information-theoretic measure of panel redundancy. Mitigation strategies include DBEI-based reweighting, Bayesian calibration, and credibility scoring \\citep{ebrahimi2025adversary}.\n\n\\paragraph{Information-Theoretic Evaluation.}\nInformation-theoretic approaches address statistical validity \\citep{robertson2025stepwise,feng2026noisy}, attack costs \\citep{kaneko2025bits}, sequential selection \\citep{jung2026logarithmic}, and truthfulness \\citep{chen2025incentivizing,qiu2026truthfulness}, but assume honest judges. We address adversarial manipulation interacting with multi-judge composition.\n",
        "numLines": 20,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 20
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/related_work.tex"
  },

_source line 2059 · 2026-05-24T19:23:38.900Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01MSDzyNnoZmrkWEwjz3fUGB`</summary>

```text
10	\caption{Panel-level correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ at $K{=}5$ ($n{=}3{,}003$ panels). Full model pool (gray, $r{=}0.81$) exhibits strong collinearity; capability-matched Pool~A (orange, $r{=}0.50$, 126 panels) shows reduced confounding.}
11	\label{fig:keff_eta_scatter}
12	\end{figure}
13	
14	\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound: A Fundamental Limitation.}
15	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ correlate at $r{=}0.70$--$0.81$ (variance inflation factor, VIF${\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; Figure~\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \citep{cinelli2020making} shows the confound explains the association in 69\% of conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}), though fractional logit confirms the positive association (9/12, 11/12, 12/12 at $K{=}3,5,7$). Pool~A ($r{=}0.50$) raises the robustness value (RV) in 10/12 at $K{=}5$ despite max 30.1\% power (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}); cross-benchmark instrumental variable (IV) analysis provides additional identification (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_benchmark}), though causal attribution requires capability-controlled designs.
16	
17	\paragraph{API-Only vs.\ Full Pool.}
18	Restricting to 9 API models removes the AWQ confound but increases collinearity ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$), reversing the pattern: $\eta_{\max}$ retains significance (8/12) while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ drops (2--3/12; Table~\ref{tab:robustness_checks}). The six local AWQ models (25--54\% accuracy) drive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ variation but also introduce the confound (Appendix~\ref{app:random_subsample}).
19	
```

_source line 2061 · 2026-05-24T19:23:37.502Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_018Yn4vh3XcHVbkn9aW8ruhf`</summary>

```text
15	
16	Controlled experiments across 15 models (9 API, 6 local quantized), $K \in \{3,5,7\}$, six attack types, and two benchmarks, Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) and the AI2 Reasoning Challenge (ARC), confirm this predictor shift. Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); adaptive attack reduces predictability to $R^2 = 0.275$ (Figure~\ref{fig:framework}).
17	
18	These findings yield three contributions:
19	\begin{enumerate}
20	\item \textbf{Diversity--vulnerability association.} $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions (12/12 under OLS and generalized additive model (GAM) bootstrap), but the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) limits causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\% of conditions (25/36), though fractional logit confirms the positive association in most conditions ($K{=}3$: 9/12; $K{=}5$: 11/12; $K{=}7$: 12/12) (\S\ref{sec:dominance}, \S\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).
21	\item \textbf{Panel-size-dependent predictor shift.} At $K{=}3$, both $\eta_{\max}$ (3/12) and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (4/12) contribute under bootstrap (co-determination); at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor (7/12 at $K{=}5$, 8/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\eta_{\max}$ sharply attenuates (2/12, then 1/12) (\S\ref{sec:cross_k}, \S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).
22	\item \textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\eta_{\max}$ persists (9/12), so the defense is partial rather than complete (\S\ref{sec:aggregation}, \S\ref{sec:analysis}).
23	\end{enumerate}
24	
```

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We document a predictor shift: at $K{=}3$, both $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ contribute; at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates (7/12, 8/12) while $\eta_{\max}$ attenuates (2/12), with positive direction (12/12) but method-sensitive significance (0--12/12; fractional logit 9/12, 11/12, 12/12). The confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; RV insufficient in 69\%) limits causal claims: screen $\eta_{\max}$ at $K{=}3$, monitor independence at $K{\geq}5$.
4

    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}\n\\label{sec:dominance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Bench.} & \\textbf{Attack} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ & \\textbf{Dom.} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Score manip.   & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nMMLU & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Position bias  & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nARC  & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Score manip.   & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Position bias  & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nARC  & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Standardized regression coefficients for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across 12 conditions (455 panels, log-log OLS). $+^{*}$/$-^{*}$: FDR-significant positive/negative (Benjamini--Hochberg, $q < 0.05$); n.s.: not significant. Dom.\\ column: $\\eta$ = $\\eta_{\\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \\citep{budescu1993dominance}.}\n\\label{tab:dominance}\n\\end{table}\n\nUnder OLS, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positive and FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions. $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotonicity}. Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels (Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_dist}).\n\nEach judge appears in $\\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with clustered SE widest ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:forest_plot}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\\\\nClustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\\\\nMixed effects          & 6/12  & 3/12  \\\\\nWild bootstrap         & 3/12  & 4/12  \\\\\nFrac.\\ logit (clust.)  & 6/12  & 9/12  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS); all methods preserve positive direction (12/12).}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis yields a minimum detectable effect (MDE) of $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (decreasing to 0.048 at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE, though positive coefficient direction (12/12) is preserved across all methods (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}).\n\n\\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Predictor Shift}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with fractional logit giving 9/12. This contrasts with $K{\\geq}5$, where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 9/12).\n\nWe repeat the wild cluster bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dominance_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-condition predictor dominance across panel sizes (wild cluster bootstrap, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$). At $K{=}3$, dominance is heterogeneous (co-dominant, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-only, $\\eta_{\\max}$-only, and non-significant conditions coexist); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates uniformly while $\\eta_{\\max}$ disappears.}\n\\label{fig:dominance_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$, with per-condition detail in Figure~\\ref{fig:dominance_heatmap}. At $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute modestly ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with co-determination in two conditions. At $K{=}5$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ drops to 2/12 while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.275$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The predictability gap narrows from ${\\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$).\n\n\\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction in all 12 conditions (88.9--100\\% positive rate; Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}), with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$, $0.423$).\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.328 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.087 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nPredictability ranges from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$); Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), yielding partial defense (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}).\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE confirms the predictor shift: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance increases from 9/12 ($K{=}3$) to 11/12 ($K{=}5$) to 12/12 ($K{=}7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.93\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{The shifting bottleneck in LLM judge panels. \\textbf{(a)}~At $K{=}3$, one compromised judge flips the majority---$\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates. \\textbf{(b)}~A predictor shift emerges at $K^* \\in (3,5)$. \\textbf{(c)}~At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise multiple independent judges---$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have become the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, an approach inspired by Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates (ASR) from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation, yet this framing conflates two distinct sources of robustness: the quality of individual judges and the independence among them.\n\nThe conflation matters because the BFT analogy breaks down in the LLM judge setting. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes from within; evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs from without, and shared pretraining corpora create correlated failure modes that violate the independence assumption underlying majority-vote guarantees \\citep{schneier2000secrets}. No existing work disentangles diversity from individual robustness across panel sizes, so the mechanism behind observed ASR reductions remains unclear.\n\nWe address this gap by decomposing panel vulnerability into two predictors: weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$, the maximum individual attack success rate in a panel) and effective independent channels ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, a spectral measure of judge independence). Across all 12 conditions (two benchmarks $\\times$ six attacks), $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s ordinary least-squares (OLS) coefficient is positive (12/12), directionally consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. However, the significance of this association is method-sensitive (0 to 12 of 12 across inference approaches), and a previously uncharacterized transition emerges: at $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute (bootstrap significance: $\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor (7/12 at $K{=}5$, 8/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\\eta_{\\max}$ sharply attenuates (2/12, then 1/12).\n\nControlled experiments across 15 models (9 API, 6 local quantized), $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, six attack types, and two benchmarks, Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) and the AI2 Reasoning Challenge (ARC), confirm this predictor shift. Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); adaptive attack reduces predictability to $R^2 = 0.275$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}).\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Diversity--vulnerability association.} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions (12/12 under OLS and generalized additive model (GAM) bootstrap), but the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) limits causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\\% of conditions (25/36), though fractional logit confirms the positive association in most conditions ($K{=}3$: 9/12; $K{=}5$: 11/12; $K{=}7$: 12/12) (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Panel-size-dependent predictor shift.} At $K{=}3$, both $\\eta_{\\max}$ (3/12) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (4/12) contribute under bootstrap (co-determination); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor (7/12 at $K{=}5$, 8/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\\eta_{\\max}$ sharply attenuates (2/12, then 1/12) (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).\n\\item \\textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), so the defense is partial rather than complete (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nOur findings are descriptive; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) limits causal claims.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 17,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 17,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{Predictor Shift and Direction.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a single compromised judge flips the majority ($t{=}2$), making both predictors relevant. At $K{\\geq}5$, no single judge is pivotal; $\\eta_{\\max}$'s contribution collapses while high-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels present more distinct attack surfaces ($K^* \\in (3,5)$). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction is positive in all 12 conditions (OLS and GAM bootstrap 12/12), but significance is method-sensitive (0--12/12; bootstrap 4/12 at $K{=}3$, 7/12 at $K{=}5$). No magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_keff_eta_scatter.pdf}\n\\caption{Panel-level correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ at $K{=}5$ ($n{=}3{,}003$ panels). Full model pool (gray, $r{=}0.81$) exhibits strong collinearity; capability-matched Pool~A (orange, $r{=}0.50$, 126 panels) shows reduced confounding.}\n\\label{fig:keff_eta_scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound: A Fundamental Limitation.}\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ correlate at $r{=}0.70$--$0.81$ (variance inflation factor, VIF${\\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; Figure~\\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \\citep{cinelli2020making} shows the confound explains the association in 69\\% of conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}), though fractional logit confirms the positive association (9/12, 11/12, 12/12 at $K{=}3,5,7$). Pool~A ($r{=}0.50$) raises the robustness value (RV) in 10/12 at $K{=}5$ despite max 30.1\\% power (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}); cross-benchmark instrumental variable (IV) analysis provides additional identification (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}), though causal attribution requires capability-controlled designs.\n\n\\paragraph{API-Only vs.\\ Full Pool.}\nRestricting to 9 API models removes the AWQ confound but increases collinearity ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$), reversing the pattern: $\\eta_{\\max}$ retains significance (8/12) while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ drops (2--3/12; Table~\\ref{tab:robustness_checks}). The six local AWQ models (25--54\\% accuracy) drive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ variation but also introduce the confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_panel_design.pdf}\n\\caption{Panel ASR under three selection strategies. $\\min(\\eta_{\\max})$ achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions, confirming robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.}\n\\label{fig:panel_design}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_aggregation_gradient.pdf}\n\\caption{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient distribution across aggregation methods ($K{=}3$). Majority and weighted voting: uniformly positive (12+/0$-$); Bayesian calibration: reduced predictability ($R^2{=}0.087$, 5+/1$-$).}\n\\label{fig:aggregation}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAggregation rules influence predictability but not $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (Figure~\\ref{fig:aggregation}); at $K{=}5$ all four methods converge to 7/12, while at $K{=}7$ Bayesian calibration neutralizes it (0/12) as the posterior concentrates on 2--3 accurate judges, compressing $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ variation (Appendices~\\ref{app:aggregation_k5},~\\ref{app:aggregation_k7}). Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in all conditions (Figure~\\ref{fig:panel_design}); clean $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is stable under 4/6 attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}).\n\n\\paragraph{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\\ Alternative Diversity Measures.}\n$\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} outperforms $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (8/12 vs.\\ 4/12) but falls behind at $K{\\geq}5$ (6--7/12 vs.\\ 7--8/12) without the predictor shift ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$; Appendix~\\ref{app:alt_diversity}). Shannon MI entropy shows no shift either ($4 \\to 4 \\to 0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:shannon_entropy}).\n",
